```json
    "family": "331",
    "text": "The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):\n\n- [Asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity and unbounded diamond distortion in a reflexive tree space](../../preprints/Asymptotic-midpoint-uniform-convexity-and-unbounded-diamond-distortion-in-a-reflexive-tree-space-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint lenses in segment spaces](../../preprints/Midpoint-lenses-in-segment-spaces-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Distortion of countably branching diamonds from midpoint and tree energies](../../preprints/Diamond-distortion-from-midpoint-and-tree-energies-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Exact asymptotic moduli in a Daugavet subspace of $L_1$](../../preprints/Exact-asymptotic-moduli-in-a-Daugavet-subspace-of-L1-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint convexity from bounded tree potentials and path costs](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-bounded-tree-potentials-and-path-costs-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Independent products in real $L_1$: asymptotic midpoint convexity without AUC renormings](../../preprints/Independent-products-in-real-L1-asymptotic-midpoint-convexity-without-AUC-renormings-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint convexity from two recursive potentials](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-two-recursive-potentials-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n\n## Scope\n\nThe paper constructs a Banach space whose midpoint geometry is asymptotically uniformly convex even though no equivalent norm is asymptotically uniformly convex in the usual one-sided sense. The formalization proves that the full dual of the specified segment-norm completion is infinite-dimensional, separable, and reflexive; its averaged midpoint modulus is at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1$ for every $t>0$, while every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity. It also proves that an embedding of a depth-$k$ countably branching diamond has distortion at least $\\sqrt{1+k/12}$.\n\nThe general-forest and word-forest results use the closed span of the coordinate functionals. Their conclusions retain the finite-ancestor and equivalent-norm hypotheses, including the scale $\\alpha/(2\\beta)$ when $0<\\alpha\\le\\beta$ and the new norm lies between $\\alpha$ and $\\beta$ times the original norm; they do not identify that span with the full dual or assert reflexivity for every forest.\n\nThe paper controls midpoint lenses in Banach spaces defined by tree-segment norms. The formalization proves the following estimate in both the full dual of the finite-height forest space and the coordinate predual of the infinite-tree space. If $x$ is supported on a finite ancestral set $H$, $R\\ge0$, and $\\|x+y\\|,\\|x-y\\|\\le R$, then the part of $y$ outside $H$ has norm at most $2\\sqrt{R^2-\\|x\\|^2}$.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected consequences for averaged midpoint moduli and separated families of vectors, together with reflexivity and renorming obstructions. This scope concerns the segment-space results; the companion results on diamond energies remain separate.\n\nThe paper studies embeddings of countably branching diamond graphs into Banach spaces built from tree segments. The formalization proves that every embedding of the depth-$k$ diamond with distortion $D$, at any positive scale, satisfies $1+k/4\\le D^2$ in either of two spaces: the full dual of the completed finite-height forest space and the closed coordinate span in the dual of the infinite-tree space. The bound applies to all pairs of vertices and is unconditional.\n\nThe formalization also contains selected graph deductions under explicit companion assumptions. Twenty-two of those companion inputs are assumed in these deductions; the diamond distortion bound above does not require them.\n\nThe paper exhibits a real $L_1$ subspace with positive averaged midpoint convexity but no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. For every closed infinite-dimensional subspace whose unit ball is precompact in measure and which has the Daugavet property, the formalization computes the moduli at every unit vector: the averaged midpoint modulus is $\\max(t/2,t-1)$ and the usual one-sided asymptotic modulus is $\\max(0,t-2)$ for every $t>0$. The same formulas hold after taking the infimum over unit vectors, and every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity.\n\nThe formalization also constructs such a subspace on a countable product of unit intervals and proves that it has the stated measure-precompactness and Daugavet properties, so the modulus formulas apply to an actual example.\n\nThe paper constructs Banach spaces from bounded tree potentials and path costs to separate averaged midpoint convexity from asymptotic uniform convexity. For the specified tree-potential completions, the formalization proves completeness, separability, infinite dimension, and an averaged midpoint modulus of at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1$ for $0<t<1$. No space linearly isomorphic to one of these completions is asymptotically uniformly convex.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected path-cost duality and comparison results, clipping and energy estimates, and further renorming obstructions. These retain their stated support, head, and tail hypotheses; the remaining auxiliary assertions of the paper are outside this scope.\n\nThe paper forms a real $L_1$ space from the closed span of products of independent multipliers along tree paths. For a positive, nonconstant multiplier of mean one and finite second moment, the formalization proves that this space is infinite-dimensional and has no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm.\n\nFor every $t>0$, the averaged midpoint modulus nevertheless has explicit positive lower bounds. If $G$ is a standard real Gaussian, the exponential-multiplier bound is $\\frac{7t}{480}\\mathbb E[(|G|-20/t)_+]$, where $u_+=\\max(u,0)$. For squared-Gaussian multipliers, the bounds are $\\frac18\\Pr(|G|\\ge4\\sqrt2(2+\\sqrt2)/t)$ and $\\frac14\\Pr(|G|\\ge8\\sqrt6/t)$.\n\nThe paper constructs three Banach spaces from recursive tree potentials: a root-sum space, a zero-root space, and a joined space. The formalization proves that all three are infinite-dimensional and have no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. The root-sum and joined spaces have averaged midpoint modulus at least $t^3/128$ for $0<t<1$, and the root-sum modulus is positive for every $t>0$. The zero-root estimate retains its finite ancestral support and tail hypotheses, and the zero-root and joined spaces are reflexive.\n\nThe formalization also covers a separated-midpoint result for the joined space and a variable-exponent model with its recursive norm, duality, projections, renorming obstruction, and sixth- and third-order stability estimates. The separation, sequence, support, and head assumptions in these results are essential parts of their statements."
  },
  "status": "present",
  "toolchain": "leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1"
}
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We derive quantitative distortion bounds for countably branching diamond graphs from midpoint estimates and direct tree energies. In the dual of a finite-height segment forest, every distortion-<i>D</i> embedding of the depth-<i>k</i> diamond satisfies $`D^2\ge1+k/4`$. The same bound holds in the infinite-height coordinate predual. We also obtain power-type distortion bounds for path-cost and recursive tree norms.

<a id="met:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Countably branching diamond graphs test whether a Banach space can accommodate many separated approximate midpoints at every scale. Each edge is replaced by countably many two-edge paths with common endpoints. A target space that forces these midpoint families away from the sphere must lose some edge stretch at every replacement. The question is what quantity decreases, and how the resulting distortion bound depends on the geometry used to prove that decrease.

We use the normalized diamond $\mathcal D_k$: the original edge has length one, and the two edges in each replacement path have half the length of their parent. Thus $\mathcal D_k$ has finite depth and countably many vertices; write $d_k$ for its shortest-path metric. A map $f$ has distortion at most $D$ if some $s>0$ satisfies $$s d_k(u,v)\le\|f(u)-f(v)\|\le Ds d_k(u,v)
 \qquad(u,v\in\mathcal D_k).$$ The scale $s$ may depend on the map and on $k$. Its removal by multiplying $f$ by a scalar does not change $D$.

Our main example applies James’s disjoint-segment norm (James 1974, 739) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[James1974](#ref-James1974)] to a forest of finite heights. Let $\mathcal T=\coprod_{h\ge1}\mathbb N^{\le h}$, with the prefix order within each rooted tree. A segment is the set of consecutive vertices between two comparable nodes. Complete the finitely supported real arrays on $\mathcal T$ in the norm $$\rho(a)=\sup\left\{
  \left(\sum_{j=1}^m\left|\sum_{s\in S_j}a_s\right|^2\right)^{1/2}:
  S_1,\ldots,S_m\text{ are pairwise disjoint segments}\right\},$$ and call the resulting space $J$. We consider $X=J^*$ with its given dual norm. On the infinite tree $T_\infty=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, including its root, let $J_\infty$ be the completion of the finitely supported real arrays for the same disjoint-segment formula. Define $$B_\infty=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{e_t^*:t\in T_\infty\}
 \subset J_\infty^*,$$ where $e_t^*$ is coordinate evaluation and the closure uses the dual norm. The companion on midpoint lenses proves the midpoint estimates and the finite-coordinate approximation facts for these two spaces (OpenAI 2026c) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)]. Section <a href="#dir:forest-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:forest-definition">3</a> also gives the forest foundations needed for our direct proofs.

James’s original construction uses the infinite binary tree. Girardi proved asymptotic uniform convexity for the coordinate predual and the dual of that binary-tree space (Girardi 2001, Theorems 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Girardi2001](#ref-Girardi2001)]. The countably branching spaces defined here use their own segment estimates.

<a id="stmt-met-joint-distortion"></a>
<div id="met:joint-distortion" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *<span id="met:intro-main" label="met:intro-main"></span>For every integer $k\ge0$, every distortion-$D$ embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ into either $X$ or $B_\infty$ satisfies $$D^2\ge1+k/4.$$*

</div>

In both cases the input is the following tail inequality. A finite set $H$ is ancestral if it contains every ancestor of each of its vertices; $P_H$ retains its coordinates and $Q_H=I-P_H$. If $P_Hp=p$, then $$\|p\pm y\|\le R
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|Q_Hy\|\le2\sqrt{R^2-\|p\|^2}.$$ It holds for arbitrary displacements $y$. In a symmetric lens $\{z:\|x\pm z\|\le R\}$ containing infinitely many separated deviations, two projected deviations can be made arbitrarily close, and their half-difference remains in the same lens. This turns the tail inequality into a loss in squared edge stretch. Section <a href="#met:joint-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:joint-section">2.3</a> gives the complete deduction. The coefficient $1/4$ results from retaining the full quadratic budget in this calculation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Midpoint methods and earlier work

Diamond graphs connect metric embeddings with convexity of Banach-space norms. For binary diamonds, Lee and Naor obtain quantitative lower bounds in $L_p$, $1<p\le2$, by summing a convexity inequality over the replacement levels (Lee and Naor 2004, Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LeeNaor2004](#ref-LeeNaor2004)]. Johnson and Schechtman characterize superreflexivity by the failure of uniformly bounded binary-diamond embeddings; their lower-bound proof iterates a modulus-of-convexity estimate (Johnson and Schechtman 2009, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[JohnsonSchechtman2009](#ref-JohnsonSchechtman2009)]. The countably branching graphs studied here make a further selection possible: infinitely many midpoint images can be compared after their projections onto a fixed finite-dimensional space have become arbitrarily close.

Dilworth, Kutzarova, Randrianarivony, Revalski, and Zhivkov introduced asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity and characterized it by uniform smallness of the noncompactness of symmetric approximate-midpoint sets (Dilworth et al. 2016, Theorem 2.1 and Definition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. In finite-codimensional terms, for each $t>0$ there is $\delta>0$ such that every unit center $x$ admits a finite-codimensional subspace on whose unit directions $y$ one has $\max\{\|x+ty\|,\|x-ty\|\}\ge1+\delta$. Replacing the maximum by $\|x+ty\|$ gives asymptotic uniform convexity. Baudier, Causey, Dilworth, Kutzarova, Randrianarivony, Schlumprecht, and Zhang turn this property into a countably branching diamond obstruction (F. Baudier et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. Their Theorem 4.1 includes a quantitative conclusion: a midpoint modulus bounded below by a positive multiple of $t^p$ for $0<t<1$, with $1<p<\infty$, gives distortion bounded below by a positive multiple of $k^{1/p}$. Their proof combines control of midpoint sets with an iterated improvement of endpoint distances. The slope-loss arguments here use that mechanism and retain the constants furnished by the stated vector estimates. Malthaner gives another proof of the power-type lower bound through coloring, a path lemma, and separated approximate-midpoint configurations (Malthaner 2025, Theorem 3.5 of arXiv v2) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Malthaner2025](#ref-Malthaner2025)]. He also proves a power-type obstruction for metric targets satisfying a diamond inequality (Malthaner 2025, Equation (2) and Theorem 3.7 of arXiv v2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Malthaner2025](#ref-Malthaner2025)]. The squared-lens inequality used here is its $p=2$ Banach-space form; our coefficient comes from the stated norm-specific tail estimate.

Converse embedding results use additional geometry. For reflexive spaces with unconditional asymptotic structure, Baudier et al. characterize existence of an equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm by the failure of uniformly bounded diamond embeddings (F. Baudier et al. 2017, Corollary 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. Swift extends the characterization to countably branching bundle graphs with these hypotheses (Swift 2018, Corollary 3.4 and Theorem 6.9) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Swift2018](#ref-Swift2018)]. Perreau gives a dual characterization for separable $Y$ when $Y^*$ has weak-star unconditional asymptotic structure, and a characterization for reflexive spaces with unconditional asymptotic structure (Perreau 2021, Theorems 4.8 and 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Perreau2021](#ref-Perreau2021)]. These assumptions remain part of the corresponding converses.

Normalized diamond slopes also occur in the sprawling-tree construction of Basset, Lancien, and Procházka (Basset, Lancien, and Procházka 2026, Definition 3.7 and Proposition 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BassetLancienProchazka2026](#ref-BassetLancienProchazka2026)]. Our direct proofs specialize that construction to finite height and then supply coordinate selections, primal corrections, and predictions of atomic segment coefficients for the norms considered here.

We distinguish numerical conventions for the midpoint modulus. Dilworth et al. (Dilworth et al. 2016, Definition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)] and Baudier et al. (F. Baudier et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)] use the maximum of the two signed norms. The Baudier–Lancien book manuscript uses their average (F. P. Baudier and Lancien 2026, Definition 5.1.15 of arXiv v1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[BaudierLancien2025](#ref-BaudierLancien2025)]. An average lower bound implies the corresponding maximum lower bound; each application below specifies which estimate it uses.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Direct proofs and the organization of the paper

The forest also admits direct graph arguments that construct their own energy budgets. These proofs use the segment norm itself, without invoking the companion’s midpoint inequality. The common first step is to select one replacement path at each retained edge. Infinite branching permits coordinate choices at this step; subsequent descent uses only the two half-edges of each selected path.

Sections <a href="#dir:forest-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:forest-definition">3</a> and <a href="#dir:accumulated" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:accumulated">4</a> develop the forest foundations and two primal-test constructions. A test is a finitely supported vector in $J$, evaluated by the current slope in $X$. One construction adds a correction at the first vertices outside a finite head, so that a gain in evaluation costs only a bounded increase in the squared test norm. The other balances prefix sums at each dyadic size and bounds the final accumulated test by charging segment sums to their largest encountered size. Both compare growing evaluations with the norm of the test.

Section <a href="#dir:atomic:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:section">5</a> uses a different budget. Fair choices between the two half-edges make the selected slopes a finite martingale. After projection to a common finite head, the terminal slopes are represented exactly by random sums of disjoint-segment functionals whose squared coefficients sum to at most one. As more of a segment’s location is revealed, conditional predictions of its coefficient have bounded total squared change. Counting segment endpoints, or only segment starts, finds an interval of levels where this prediction energy cannot sustain all the large new coordinate layers selected from the graph. The coordinate selections and test estimates here are specific to our norm.

Section <a href="#dir:layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:layers">6</a> then treats the linear path-cost norm. It states each imported energy estimate alongside the graph construction that meets its support and timing hypotheses. Some constructions keep the exact graph slopes and allow small tails outside a head; others replace the slopes by finitely supported approximations while preserving their exact averaging identity.

Section <a href="#met:transfer-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:transfer-section">7</a> collects the remaining midpoint transfers after these direct proofs. They distinguish squared or higher-power losses, a fixed contraction from a separated lens, and growth of an actual child slope. Sections <a href="#met:d-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:d-section">8</a> and <a href="#met:f:applications" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:f:applications">9</a> introduce the other path-cost, segment-start and recursive-array norms and apply these transfers. Each application states its intrinsic input from the complete companions on bounded tree potentials or recursive potentials. Linear and quadratic path costs, free and fixed joining roots, and different child-aggregation exponents give different norms; the stated constants belong to those specified norms and inputs. Appendix <a href="#met:forest-variants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:forest-variants">10</a> records the alternative forest midpoint arguments and their individual quantitative or qualitative losses.

<a id="met:section"></a>
### From infinitely many midpoints to diamond distortion

A symmetric midpoint estimate becomes a graph obstruction because each edge of a countably branching diamond has infinitely many replacement midpoints. Finite-dimensional compactness lets us compare two of them while making any prescribed finite set of coordinates negligible. We give the transfer with all scaling factors and deduce the principal squared-loss bound. Section <a href="#met:transfer-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:transfer-section">7</a> develops further ways to accumulate midpoint estimates. These are instances of the midpoint method for countably branching diamonds; see (F. Baudier et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. The quantitative constants below come from the estimates stated here.

For $x\in X$ and $R\ge0$, write $\mathcal L_R(x)=\{z\in X:\|x+z\|\le R,\ \|x-z\|\le R\}$ for the symmetric $R$-lens at $x$. Its members are deviations from the common midpoint, rather than the two endpoints themselves.

<a id="met:graph-definition"></a>
#### The graph and its slopes

Let $\mathcal D_0$ consist of two vertices joined by an edge of length one. To obtain $\mathcal D_{j+1}$, replace every edge $[a,b]$ of $\mathcal D_j$ by paths $[a,m_i,b]$, indexed by $i\in\mathbb N$, whose middle vertices are distinct and new. Both new edges have half the length of their parent. Let $d_k$ be the shortest-path metric on the vertices of $\mathcal D_k$. We call an edge present at stage $j$ a *stage-$j$ edge*; it need no longer be an edge at stage $k>j$.

Distances between old vertices are unchanged at a replacement. Indeed, an old path can be replaced by paths of the same total length. Conversely, a path between old vertices consists of two-edge portions through new vertices; each portion either returns to the same old vertex or traverses the endpoints of one old edge. Compressing these portions gives an old walk of no greater length. It follows that a stage-$j$ edge has endpoint distance $\ell_j=2^{-j}$ in $\mathcal D_k$. Its distinct replacement midpoints have mutual distance $\ell_j$, and distance $\ell_j/2$ from either endpoint. At their introduction the assertion is immediate because two distinct middle vertices are nonadjacent, and every incident edge has length $\ell_j/2$; subsequent replacements preserve it.

<figure id="fig:diamond">

<figcaption>One normalized diamond replacement, with only three of the countably many paths shown. Each new edge has length $ℓ/2$. The old endpoints remain at distance $ℓ$, and any two distinct new middle vertices also have distance $ℓ$. The drawing is schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-diamond"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:diamond`)

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,every node/.style={font=\small}]

 \fill (0,0) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};
 \fill (2,0) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};
 \draw[-{Stealth},thick] (2.8,0)--(4,0);
 \coordinate (u) at (5,0); \coordinate (v) at (9,0);
 \foreach \h/\lab in {1.4/1,0.45/2,-1.4/j}{
   \draw[thick] (u)--(7,\h)--(v);
   \fill (7,\h) circle (2pt) node[above] {$w_{\lab}$};
 }
 \node at (5.75,1.0) {$\ell/2$};
 \node at (8.25,1.0) {$\ell/2$};
 \node at (7,-0.5) {$\vdots$};
 \fill (u) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};
 \fill (v) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 4 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A replacement step diagram showing on the left a single horizontal edge connecting endpoint vertices $u$ and $v$ with label $\ell$ above and label "one old edge" below. A horizontal rightward arrow connects this to the right diagram, which shows endpoints $u$ and $v$ connected by multiple two-edge paths via intermediate vertices $w_1$ (top), $w_2$ (middle), vertical ellipsis dots, and $w_j$ (bottom). The edges connecting $u$ to $w_1$ and $w_1$ to $v$ are each labeled $\ell/2$. Below the right diagram is the label "countably many two-edge paths".
```


The normalized metric $d_k$ defined above differs by the global factor $2^{-k}$ from the convention in which every edge of the final graph has length one. In that convention old-vertex distances double at each replacement and a stage-$j$ edge has represented length $2^{k-j}$. Both conventions give exactly the same embedding distortion.

<a id="met:all-pairs"></a>
Suppose $f:\mathcal D_k\to X$, $s>0$ and $D\ge1$ satisfy $$\label{met:all-pairs}
 s d_k(u,v)\le\|f(u)-f(v)\|\le Ds d_k(u,v)
 \qquad(u,v\in\mathcal D_k).$$ The lower inequality is required for all pairs, including distinct replacement midpoints. Dividing $f$ by $s$ gives the noncontracting normalization $d_k\le\|f(u)-f(v)\|\le Dd_k$. Dividing instead by $Ds$ gives lower factor $\alpha=1/D$ and upper factor one. We use both normalizations and state which one is in force.

<a id="met:slopes"></a>
Orient the original edge and orient every replacement path consistently. For a parent edge $[a,b]$ of represented length $\ell$, define $$\label{met:slopes}
 x=\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{\ell},\qquad
 z_i=\frac{2}{\ell}\left(f(m_i)-\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2\right).$$ Its child slopes are $x+z_i$ and $x-z_i$. Under lower factor $\alpha$, <a id="met:sibling-separation"></a>$$\label{met:sibling-separation}
 \|z_i-z_j\|\ge2\alpha\qquad(i\ne j).$$ Every slope lies between $\alpha$ and the upper embedding factor. The sequence $(z_i)$ is bounded, since each $z_i$ is half the difference of two child slopes.

<a id="met:pairing-section"></a>
#### The finite-dimensional pair selection

The next observation records the approximation errors that will be used throughout. It requires bounded finite-rank projections, rather than contractivity of the complementary projections.

<a id="stmt-met-finite-pair"></a>
<div id="met:finite-pair" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *Let $X$ be a Banach space, $R>0$, and let $(z_i)_{i\in\mathbb N}$ satisfy $\|x\pm z_i\|\le R$ with $\|z_i-z_j\|\ge\varepsilon>0$ for $i\ne j$. Every half-difference $w=(z_i-z_j)/2$ with $i\ne j$ satisfies $\|w\|\ge\varepsilon/2$ and $\|x\pm w\|\le R$. Fix $\eta>0$. The following two selections are available.*

<a id="met:pair-errors"></a>
1.  *Suppose $p$ has finite support, $\|p-x\|<\eta$, and $P$ is a bounded finite-rank coordinate projection satisfying $Pp=p$. There are distinct $i,j$ for which, with $w=(z_i-z_j)/2$ and $y=w-Pw$, $$\label{met:pair-errors}
     \|Pw\|<\eta,\quad
     \|y\|\ge\varepsilon/2-\eta,\quad
     \|p\|\ge\|x\|-\eta,\quad
    \|p\pm y\|\le R+2\eta.$$*

2.  *If $F$ is a closed finite-codimensional subspace, there are distinct $i,j$ whose half-difference satisfies $\operatorname{dist}(w,F)<\eta$. This selection requires no coordinate structure on $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-finite-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-finite-pair</summary>

*Proof.* For the common endpoint bound use $$x+w=\tfrac12((x+z_i)+(x-z_j)),\qquad
x-w=\tfrac12((x-z_i)+(x+z_j)).$$ In the first selection, the bounded sequence $(Pz_i)$ has two distinct members at distance less than $2\eta$, because bounded subsets of a finite-dimensional space are totally bounded. This gives the first inequality in <a href="#met:pair-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:pair-errors">[met:pair-errors]</a> and hence the second. Replacing $x$ by $p$ and $w$ by $w-Pw$ costs less than $2\eta$. The last assertion follows by applying the same argument to the bounded images of $z_i$ in the finite-dimensional quotient $X/F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:joint-section"></a>
#### The quadratic loss from the pooled atomic estimate

<a id="met:joint-input"></a>
For the segment forest space, the pooled atomic estimate has the form $$\label{met:joint-input}
 \|Q_Hy\|\le2\sqrt{R^2-\|p\|^2},
 \qquad P_Hp=p,\quad \|p\pm y\|\le R.$$ Here $H$ is finite and ancestral, $Q_H=I-P_H$, and the displacement $y$ is arbitrary. The estimate is supplied by the joint-budget theorem in the companion on midpoint lenses (OpenAI 2026c, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)]. Its target is the projected tail $Q_Hy$; an individual endpoint-law estimate is not being used with an arbitrary head.

The same companion establishes, for both the finite-forest dual and the infinite-height closed coordinate span, density of finite coordinate vectors and contractivity of $P_H$ and $Q_H$ for ancestral $H$. Thus $P_Hx$ can approximate any center $x$: take a finite coordinate approximation and let $H$ contain its support and all its ancestors. These approximation properties refer to each space with its own norm.

<a id="stmt-met-joint-lens-lemma"></a>
<div id="met:joint-lens-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="met:joint-lens"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *In the finite-height segment forest dual, every infinite $\varepsilon$-separated symmetric $R$-lens satisfies $$\label{met:joint-lens}
 \|x\|^2+\varepsilon^2/16\le R^2.$$ The same lens conclusion holds for the infinite-height closed coordinate span with its own segment-dual norm and finite-head approximation theorem.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-joint-lens-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-joint-lens-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $0<\eta<\varepsilon/2$ and choose $H$ so that $p=P_Hx$ has $\|p-x\|<\eta$. Choose two deviations with $w=(z_i-z_j)/2$ satisfying $\|P_Hw\|<\eta$. Then $\|Q_Hw\|\ge\varepsilon/2-\eta$ and $\|p\pm w\|\le R+\eta$, because $\|x\pm w\|\le R$. Apply <a href="#met:joint-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:joint-input">[met:joint-input]</a> to the arbitrary displacement $w$: $$(\varepsilon/2-\eta)^2
 \le4\bigl((R+\eta)^2-\|p\|^2\bigr).$$ Letting $\eta\downarrow0$ proves <a href="#met:joint-lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:joint-lens">[met:joint-lens]</a>. The same argument applies with the infinite-height coordinate span’s own version of <a href="#met:joint-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:joint-input">[met:joint-input]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-joint-lens-lemma-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-joint-lens-lemma</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#met:intro-main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:intro-main">[met:intro-main]</a>.* Normalize to lower factor one and upper factor $D$. Let $R_j$ be the supremum of stage-$j$ slope norms. Every parent slope is the center of a $2$-separated lens whose endpoints have norms at most $R_{j+1}$. Thus $\|x_e\|^2+1/4\le R_{j+1}^2$ for every parent. Only now take the supremum: $R_j^2+1/4\le R_{j+1}^2$. Since $R_0\ge1$ and $R_k\le D$, summing gives $D^2\ge1+k/4$. No supremum need be attained. The infinite-height statement uses exactly the same scalar argument with that space’s proved version of <a href="#met:joint-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:joint-input">[met:joint-input]</a>; no identification with the full infinite-height dual is required. ◻

</details>

The $1/4$ coefficient comes from retaining the joint quadratic budget in <a href="#met:joint-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:joint-input">[met:joint-input]</a>. The direct graph-energy arguments follow next. General transfer rules and alternative forest estimates appear in Section <a href="#met:transfer-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:transfer-section">7</a> and Appendix <a href="#met:forest-variants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:forest-variants">10</a>.

<a id="dir:forest-definition"></a>
### The segment forest and its atoms

The direct graph arguments below use the same finite-height segment forest, with James’s disjoint-segment norm (James 1974, 739) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[James1974](#ref-James1974)]. We first give its norm and the two forms of its atomic unit ball that the arguments need.

<a id="dir:atomic:forest-norm"></a>
Let $$T=\coprod_{n\ge1} T_n,\qquad T_n=\mathbb N^{\le n},$$ where each component has its own root and is ordered by extension. A segment is a set $[a,b]=\{t:a\le t\le b\}$ with $a\le b$ in one component. Disjoint segments need only be disjoint as sets. A set is ancestral if it contains every ancestor of each of its vertices. On real finitely supported arrays define $$\label{dir:atomic:forest-norm}
 \rho(y)=
 \sup_{\mathcal I}
 \left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}\left|\sum_{t\in I}y(t)\right|^2\right)^{1/2},$$ where the supremum runs over finite disjoint families of segments. Let $J$ be its completion and $X=J^*$. Write $e_t^*$ for the coordinate functional and $s_I^*=\sum_{t\in I}e_t^*$, with $s_\varnothing^*=0$. We recall the elementary facts that the argument needs, including the exact form of the atomic representation.

<a id="stmt-dir-atomic-atoms"></a>
<div id="dir:atomic:atoms" class="lemma">

<a id="dir:atomic:atom-bound"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *The spaces $J$ and $X$ are reflexive, and finite coordinate arrays are dense in both. If a set $B\subset T$ meets each segment in either a segment or the empty set, its coordinate projection $P_B$ is contractive on both spaces. For an antichain $V$, the coordinate space supported on $V$ has its $\ell_2(V)$ norm in both spaces. In particular, $$\label{dir:atomic:atom-bound}
 \left\|\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}\lambda_I s_I^*\right\|_X
 \le\left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}\lambda_I^2\right)^{1/2}$$ for every disjoint segment family. Call a vector on the left with $\sum_I\lambda_I^2\le1$ an atom. The unit ball of $X$ is the norm-closed convex hull of the atoms. If $S$ is finite and ancestral, the unit ball of $P_SX$ is the convex hull of atoms whose segments lie in $S$; every point has a finite convex representation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-atomic-atoms"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-atomic-atoms</summary>

*Proof.* Singleton tests give the lower estimate in $$\|y\|_2\le\rho(y)\le\sqrt{n+1}\,\|y\|_2
 \qquad(\operatorname{supp}y\subset T_n).$$ For the upper estimate apply Cauchy–Schwarz on every segment and use its length at most $n+1$, followed by disjointness. Norm squares add over the components: one inequality follows by restricting each segment family to each component, and the reverse inequality follows by combining families that approximate the component suprema. Thus $J$ is the $\ell_2$-sum of spaces isomorphic to $\ell_2(T_n)$. The dual of an $\ell_2$-sum is the $\ell_2$-sum of the duals: Cauchy–Schwarz gives the upper norm bound and finite coordinate tests give the reverse bound. Applying this description to the bidual proves reflexivity, and truncation first in components and then in coordinates proves density in $J$ and $X$.

Intersecting a disjoint segment family with $B$ proves contractivity on $J$, hence on $X$ by duality. This applies to ancestral sets, their complements, differences of nested ancestral sets, and antichains. Each segment meets an antichain in at most one point, so singleton tests identify its $\rho$ norm with $\ell_2$; duality and the contractive projection give the same identification in $X$. Equation <a href="#dir:atomic:atom-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atom-bound">[dir:atomic:atom-bound]</a> follows directly from Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#dir:atomic:forest-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:forest-norm">[dir:atomic:forest-norm]</a>.

The supremum of pairing with the atoms equals the right side of <a href="#dir:atomic:forest-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:forest-norm">[dir:atomic:forest-norm]</a>. If their norm-closed convex hull omitted a point of the unit ball, Hahn–Banach separation would give a separating functional in $X^*=J$, contradicting this equality. Here reflexivity is used to identify the separating functional with an element of $J$. For finite ancestral $S$, the same argument is finite-dimensional: projection clips atoms to atoms in $S$, and the family of atoms in $S$ is compact, being a finite union of continuous images of finite-dimensional Euclidean unit balls. Its convex hull is compact and equals the coordinate unit ball. Every point therefore has a finite convex representation. ◻

</details>

For ancestral $S$ put $Q_S=I-P_S$. Finite-support density and contractivity imply that, for a finite collection of vectors and any $\eta>0$, a finite ancestral $S$ makes every $Q_S$-tail smaller than $\eta$. Indeed, take finite approximations and add all ancestors of their supports; each vertex has only finitely many ancestors.

<a id="dir:accumulated"></a>
### Direct descent with primal tests

The next two arguments follow actual edges of a diamond and construct a test vector at each step. Earlier diamond obstructions include Lee–Naor’s telescoping squared-distance inequality (Lee and Naor 2004, Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[LeeNaor2004](#ref-LeeNaor2004)] and Johnson–Schechtman’s iterated modulus-of-convexity estimate (Johnson and Schechtman 2009, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[JohnsonSchechtman2009](#ref-JohnsonSchechtman2009)]. Here the accumulated evaluation is compared with the cost of its primal test. The first argument pays a bounded squared-norm cost for each increase in its evaluation. The second balances prefix sums of comparable sizes and controls the final test all at once.

<a id="dir:graph-slopes"></a>
#### The graph slopes

<a id="dir:splitting"></a>
For an oriented parent edge of represented length $\ell$, use the slope $x$ and deviations $z_j$ of Section <a href="#met:graph-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:graph-definition">2.1</a>: $$\label{dir:splitting}
 x=\frac{f(b)-f(a)}\ell,\qquad
 z_j=\frac{2f(m_j)-f(a)-f(b)}\ell,\qquad
 \text{child slopes }x+z_j,\ x-z_j.$$ In the noncontracting normalization all slopes have norm at most $D$ and the deviations are $2$-separated. After division by $D$, these bounds are one and $2/D$. The symmetric splitting is the finite-level version of the sprawling-tree construction in (Basset, Lancien, and Procházka 2026, Definition 3.7 and Proposition 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BassetLancienProchazka2026](#ref-BassetLancienProchazka2026)].

The selections below use the same compactness step as Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>: two bounded projections onto a fixed finite head can be made arbitrarily close. Their separated full vectors then have a large tail difference, so at least one has a large tail. This choice requires the infinitely many middle vertices. Once a middle vertex has been selected, either sign in <a href="#dir:splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:splitting">[dir:splitting]</a> follows an actual child edge.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### A correction at the first fresh roots

The correction controls the extra squared cost of segments crossing the head boundary. Its value is placed at each first exterior vertex. For crossings with a large head sum, the proof either makes the cross term nonpositive or controls it by splitting the head portion.

<a id="stmt-dir-root-correction"></a>
<div id="dir:root-correction" class="lemma">

<a id="dir:correction-cost"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *Suppose $v,u\in c_{00}(T)$, $v=P_Av$, and $u=Q_Au$, where $A$ is finite and ancestral. There is $c=Q_Ac\in c_{00}(T)$ such that $$\label{dir:correction-cost}
 \rho(c)\le\rho(u),\qquad
 \rho(v+c+r u)^2\le\rho(v)^2+16\rho(u)^2
 \quad(r\in\{-1,1\}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-root-correction"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-root-correction</summary>

*Proof.* The complement of $A$ is the disjoint union of subtrees $U_d$ whose roots are its first vertices. Put $u_d=u|_{U_d}$ and $a_d=\rho(u_d)$. Independent segment tests in these subtrees give $\sum_d a_d^2=\rho(u)^2$; only finitely many terms are nonzero. If $d$ has parent $p\in A$, examine the suffix sums $V_d(h)=\sum_{q\in[h,p]}v(q)$. If some suffix has absolute sum greater than $2a_d$, choose the shortest such suffix, with initial vertex $h_d$, and set $$c_d=-\operatorname{sign}(V_d(h_d))a_d.$$ Otherwise set $c_d=0$, also when $d$ has no parent in $A$. Let $c=\sum_dc_de_d$. The roots $d$ are incomparable, so $\rho(c)^2=\sum_dc_d^2\le\rho(u)^2$.

Fix a disjoint segment family testing $v+c+ru$. Segments wholly outside $A$ cost at most $4\sum_da_d^2$ in squared sum, since $\rho(c_de_d+ru_d)\le2a_d$. A crossing into $U_d$ has head sum $P$ and tail sum $Q$, with $|Q|\le2a_d$. We claim that $$(P+Q)^2\le E+12a_d^2,$$ where $E$ is the squared-sum test of one or two disjoint subsegments of the original head portion. If $|P|\le2a_d$, use $E=P^2$, since $2|PQ|+Q^2\le12a_d^2$. Otherwise the preferred suffix exists. If its sum has the same sign as $P$, the choice of $c_d$ gives $PQ\le0$, and again $E=P^2$ suffices. In the remaining case let $P'=V_d(h_d)$. The preferred suffix lies properly inside the head portion: the latter qualifies, and the former is shortest and has the opposite sign. Splitting there gives $$E=(P-P')^2+(P')^2\ge P^2+4|P|a_d\ge P^2+2PQ.$$ Adding $Q^2\le4a_d^2$ proves the claim in this case as well. All the replacement head segments remain disjoint. Their total squared test, including the unchanged segments in $A$, is at most $\rho(v)^2$. At most one crossing uses each fresh root $d$. Combining the crossing cost $12\sum_da_d^2$ and the wholly exterior cost proves <a href="#dir:correction-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:correction-cost">[dir:correction-cost]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-dir-deterministic-descent"></a>
<div id="dir:deterministic-descent" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6**. *Every embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ into $X$ with distortion at most $D$ satisfies $k\le256D^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-deterministic-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-deterministic-descent</summary>

*Proof.* Rescale to upper Lipschitz constant one and lower constant $1/D$. Start at the initial edge with test $v=0$. At a current edge of slope $x$, enlarge a finite ancestral $A$ to contain the support of $v$ and make $\|Q_Ax\|<1/(4D)$. In <a href="#dir:splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:splitting">[dir:splitting]</a>, choose two deviations whose head projections are less than $1/(2D)$ apart. Since the deviations are $2/D$-separated, at least one of them, denoted $z$, has $\|Q_Az\|>1/(2D)$. Finite-support density in $J$ and contractivity of $Q_A$ supply $u=Q_Au\in c_{00}(T)$ with $\rho(u)\le1$ and $z(u)>1/(2D)$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#dir:root-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:root-correction">5</a>. Because $\rho(c)\le1$, $|x(c)|<1/(4D)$. Averaging the two matching evaluations gives $$\frac12\sum_{r=\pm1}(x+rz)(v+c+ru)
 =x(v)+x(c)+z(u)>x(v)+\frac1{4D}.$$ Choose a sign attaining at least this average, follow that actual child edge, and replace $v$ by $v+c+ru$. Each step increases $\rho(v)^2$ by at most $16$. After $k$ steps the evaluation is at least $k/(4D)$, whereas the final slope has norm at most one and the final test has norm at most $4\sqrt{k}$. Hence $k\le256D^2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Balancing dyadic sizes along a random descent

The next construction does not control the increase of the test norm at an individual step. It controls prefix sums separately at every dyadic size, then bounds any segment by charging its largest size to a root through which that segment passes.

<a id="stmt-dir-bin-descent"></a>
<div id="dir:bin-descent" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7**. *Every noncontracting $D$-Lipschitz embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ into $X$ satisfies $D>\sqrt{k}/144$ for $k\ge1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-bin-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-bin-descent</summary>

<a id="dir:bin-witness"></a>
*Proof.* We descend at random, choosing a fair sign after selecting a middle vertex. Write $x$ for the current slope and $G$ for the accumulated test, initially zero. Along a fixed history, the preceding tests lie in finite blocks $Q\setminus P$ with ancestral endpoints, and every new $P$ contains the previous $Q$. Enlarge $P$ until $\|Q_Px\|<1/32$. By the separation in <a href="#dir:splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:splitting">[dir:splitting]</a> and finite-dimensional total boundedness, choose a deviation $z$ with $\|Q_Pz\|\ge3/4$. There is a finite test $g$ supported in a block $Q\setminus P$ such that $$\label{dir:bin-witness}
 \rho(g)\le1,\qquad z(g)>1/2.$$ Here $Q$ is a finite ancestral enlargement containing the support of $g$; both choices are made before tossing the next sign.

Decompose the block into its connected tree components $C$. If $r_C$ is the root of $C$, define $$p_C(v)=\sum_{w\in[r_C,v]}g(w),\qquad
 A_C=\max_{v\in C}|p_C(v)|.$$ Ignore components with $A_C=0$. Choose a power of two $B_C$ with $A_C\le B_C<2A_C$. Disjoint root-prefix tests give $\sum_CA_C^2\le1$. Differencing the potentials $\max(p_C,0)$ and $\min(p_C,0)$ gives arrays $g_C^+,g_C^-$ with $g|_C=g_C^++g_C^-$. On every subsegment, either array has sum in absolute value at most the absolute value of the corresponding sum of $g$, since both scalar truncations are 1-Lipschitz.

To state the balancing rule precisely, call a component earlier if it was selected before the current update. Label each earlier component by its bin size and, for every vertex $v$ and dyadic size $B$, put $$S_B(v)=\sum_{\substack{t\preceq v:\ t\text{ lies in an earlier}\\
                              \text{component of bin size }B}}G(t).$$ The invariant is $|S_B(v)|\le B$ for every $v$. Before adding a new component $C$, the sum of earlier bin-$B_C$ contributions above its root $r_C$ is fixed; choose a weak sign opposite to this sum, with either choice at zero. Allow the new test on $C$ to be zero or either of $g_C^+,g_C^-$ oriented so that all its prefix sums have this chosen sign. Each such prefix has magnitude at most $B_C$.

This preserves the invariant at every vertex. All earlier supports lie in $P$, so their prefix contribution is constant through $C$. Adding an opposite-sign quantity of magnitude at most $B_C$ keeps its absolute value at most $B_C$. In a gap there is no new contribution; an ignored component has $g|_C=0$. Beyond $Q$, the new prefix is the one at its last ancestor in $Q$, or zero when there is no such ancestor. Thus the same bound holds after every update, for every ancestor path.

The sign restriction is fixed before the next fair sign $s\in\{-1,1\}$. For each $C$, choose an allowed option maximizing $s z$ on that component; let $w$ be the resulting increment. The choice depends on $s$. Nevertheless $\rho(w)\le1$: every tested segment meets this block in a segment in one component, and the preceding contraction estimate applies simultaneously to disjoint tests. If $a,b$ are the evaluations under $z$ of the two oriented offers in a component, then $$\mathbb E_s\max(0,sa,sb)
 \ge\frac{|a|+|b|}{4}
 =\frac{|z(g_C^+)|+|z(g_C^-)|}{4}
 \ge\frac{z(g|_C)}4.$$ Sum over $C$ and use <a href="#dir:bin-witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:bin-witness">[dir:bin-witness]</a> to obtain $\mathbb E_s[s z(w)]>1/8$. Also $|x(w)|<1/32$ and $\mathbb E_s[s z(G)]=0$. Thus the next actual slope $x+sz$ satisfies $$\mathbb E_s[(x+sz)(G+w)]>x(G)+1/8-1/32>x(G)+1/16.$$ After $k$ steps the expected evaluation exceeds $k/16$. The entire probability space has only $2^k$ outcomes.

It remains to bound $\rho(G)$ for every final outcome. Fix any finite disjoint segment family. On a segment, first separate the portion lying in the component that contains its initial vertex, if this is a nonignored component. Across all segments these initial portions have squared-sum total at most $k$: grouping them by construction step gives disjoint tests of an increment of norm at most one. The remainder of a segment enters every nonignored component it meets at its root, since all blocks have ancestral endpoints and are successively outside previous blocks. For a bin of size $B$, its sum on this remainder is a difference of two values of $S_B$, hence has magnitude at most $2B$. A remainder meeting no nonignored component contributes zero. Otherwise, if $B_*$ is the largest encountered bin, geometric summation gives a total magnitude at most $4B_*$. Charge the square of this bound to an encountered component with size $B_*$. Two disjoint segments cannot charge the same component: both would contain its root. Since $$\sum_{\text{all components}}B_C^2
 \le4\sum_{\text{steps}}\sum_CA_C^2\le4k,$$ the remaining portions have squared-sum total at most $64k$. The Euclidean triangle inequality for the vector of segment sums now gives $\rho(G)\le\sqrt{k}+8\sqrt{k}=9\sqrt{k}$.

Every final slope has norm at most $D$, so every final evaluation is at most $9D\sqrt{k}$. Comparison with its expectation gives $k/16<9D\sqrt{k}$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="dir:atomic:section"></a>
### Direct graph obstructions from atomic prediction energy

We give two direct proofs that the countably branching diamonds do not embed with uniformly bounded distortion into the finite-height segment-forest dual. Both proofs first select finitely many edge slopes from the infinitely branching graph. An atomic representation then turns these slopes into conditional means. The first proof counts segment endpoints and predicts the coefficients of segments crossing a long interval of layers. The second counts segment starts and follows each segment until it terminates. Their energy estimates concern these finite martingales; no midpoint-modulus estimate is needed.

<a id="stmt-dir-atomic-obstruction"></a>
<div id="dir:atomic:obstruction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 8**. *For the real space $X$ defined by <a href="#dir:atomic:forest-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:forest-norm">[dir:atomic:forest-norm]</a>, no finite $D$ has the following property: for every $k\ge1$ there are a map $f_k:\mathcal D_k\to X$ and a scalar $a_k>0$ such that $$a_k d_k(u,v)\le\|f_k(u)-f_k(v)\|_X\le D a_k d_k(u,v)
 \qquad(u,v\in\mathcal D_k).$$*

</div>

<a id="dir:atomic:normalization"></a>
<a id="dir:atomic:splittings"></a>
Rescaling by $D a_k$ reduces the theorem to excluding maps satisfying $$\label{dir:atomic:normalization}
 D^{-1}d_k(u,v)\le\|f(u)-f(v)\|_X\le d_k(u,v).$$ Necessarily $D\ge1$. The graph normalization and old-vertex distance calculation in Section <a href="#met:graph-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:graph-definition">2.1</a> apply throughout. Orient the edges consistently and write their normalized slopes as in <a href="#met:slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:slopes">[met:slopes]</a>. They have norm at most one, and each retained parent has the symmetric children $$\label{dir:atomic:splittings}
 x+d_j,\quad x-d_j,\qquad
 \|d_j-d_l\|_X\ge 2/D\quad(j\ne l).$$

At every retained edge choose one replacement path, retain both its slopes, and then descend by fair binary choices. The resulting random slopes $M_0,\ldots,M_k$ form a martingale, because the two children average to their parent. Let $\mathcal F_i$ record the first $i$ binary choices. Every history at level $i$ has probability $2^{-i}$, and each history has two children of conditional probability $1/2$. Infinite branching is used only to select a suitable replacement path before this finite probability space is formed.

<a id="stmt-dir-atomic-preparation"></a>
<div id="dir:atomic:preparation" class="lemma">

<a id="dir:atomic:preparation-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 9** (A finite slope martingale and its exact atomic law). *Suppose a depth-$k$ embedding satisfies <a href="#dir:atomic:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:normalization">[dir:atomic:normalization]</a>. For every $0<\eta<1/(4D)$ one can select a fair binary slope martingale $(M_i)_{i=0}^k$, nested finite ancestral sets $S_0\subseteq\cdots\subseteq S_k$, and a finitely valued random atom $Z$ supported in $S_k$, such that, with $E_i=\mathbb E[\,\cdot\mid\mathcal F_i]$ and $U_i=E_iZ=P_{S_k}M_i$, $$\label{dir:atomic:preparation-bounds}
 \|Q_{S_{i-1}}U_{i-1}\|<\eta,\qquad
 \|P_{S_i\setminus S_{i-1}}U_i\|\ge1/D-4\eta
 \quad(1\le i\le k).$$ The unprojected slopes also satisfy $\|Q_{S_i}M_i\|<\eta$ for $0\le i\le k$. Every $M_i$ and $U_i$ has norm at most one. The additional terminal sampling used to produce $Z$ is excluded from each $\mathcal F_i$, including $\mathcal F_k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-atomic-preparation"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-atomic-preparation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a finite ancestral $S_0$ with $\|Q_{S_0}M_0\|<\eta$. Suppose that the selected level-$(i-1)$ slopes have tails smaller than $\eta$ outside a common finite ancestral $S_{i-1}$. For each such parent $x$, the first-child slopes $x+d_j$ in <a href="#dir:atomic:splittings" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:splittings">[dir:atomic:splittings]</a> have bounded projections to the finite-dimensional space $P_{S_{i-1}}X$. Two distinct projections are within $2\eta$. Their tail difference has norm at least $2/D-2\eta$, so one first-child tail has norm at least $1/D-\eta$. Retain that path. Since its two slopes add to $2x$, the mate’s tail has norm at least $1/D-3\eta$.

Only finitely many slopes have now been selected. Enlarge $S_{i-1}$ to a finite ancestral $S_i$ making all their tails smaller than $\eta$. Writing $B_i=S_i\setminus S_{i-1}$, both children satisfy $$\|P_{B_i}M_i\|\ge1/D-4\eta.$$ Repeat through level $k$. All sets are common to an entire level, not chosen after its final random history.

The finite-support martingale $P_{S_k}M_i$ has norm at most one. By Lemma <a href="#dir:atomic:atoms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atoms">4</a>, at every terminal binary history its value is a finite convex combination of atoms supported in $S_k$. Enlarge the finite sample space by selecting one atom from that combination and call it $$Z=\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}a_I s_I^*,\qquad \sum_{I\in\mathcal I}a_I^2\le1.$$ The extra terminal sampling is excluded from every $\mathcal F_i$, even $\mathcal F_k$. With $E_i=\mathbb E[\,\cdot\mid\mathcal F_i]$, the tower property gives $E_iZ=P_{S_k}M_i$. The projections commute, and $S_i\subseteq S_k$, so the displayed layer lower bounds also hold for $U_i$. Moreover, $Q_{S_{i-1}}U_{i-1}=P_{S_k}Q_{S_{i-1}}M_{i-1}$; contractivity gives the first bound. This proves <a href="#dir:atomic:preparation-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:preparation-bounds">[dir:atomic:preparation-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

The lemma supplies the same finite martingale for two different energy arguments. In either argument a large child layer is decomposed into three terms: segments charged to the chosen interval, coefficients already predicted from the parent, and the change in their prediction. The first term has a counting budget, the second is controlled by the small parent tail, and scalar $L^2$ orthogonality bounds the third.

<a id="dir:atomic:endpoint-route"></a>
#### Endpoint intervals and crossing predictions

<a id="dir:atomic:endpoint-layer"></a>
We prove Theorem <a href="#dir:atomic:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:obstruction">8</a> first by charging the two endpoints of every terminal atom. Assume that <a href="#dir:atomic:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:normalization">[dir:atomic:normalization]</a> is available for every $k$, with $D$ fixed, and set <a id="dir:atomic:endpoint-constants"></a>$$\label{dir:atomic:endpoint-constants}
 \eta=\frac1{32D},\qquad c=\frac1{2D}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#dir:atomic:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:preparation">9</a> with this $\eta$, write $B_i=S_i\setminus S_{i-1}$, and write its random atom as $$Z=\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}w_I s_I^*,\qquad \sum_Iw_I^2\le1.$$ Since $1/D-4\eta>c$, we have on every binary history $$\label{dir:atomic:endpoint-layer}
 \|Q_{S_{i-1}}E_{i-1}Z\|<\eta,\qquad
 \|P_{B_i}E_iZ\|\ge c
 \quad(1\le i\le k).$$

<a id="dir:atomic:endpoint-error"></a>
Take $k=ML$ and partition the layers into $M$ consecutive intervals of length $L$. A segment has at most two endpoints, so the sum, over these intervals, of its squared coefficient charged to an interval containing an endpoint is at most $2w_I^2$. Consequently some interval $p\le i\le q$, $q-p+1=L$, has $$\mathbb E\sum_{\substack{I:\text{an endpoint of }I\text{ lies in}
                   \,S_q\setminus S_{p-1}}}w_I^2\le\frac2M.$$ Call these segments endpoint segments and let $Z^{\rm end}$ be their sum. Put $b_0=\sqrt{2/M}$. By <a href="#dir:atomic:atom-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atom-bound">[dir:atomic:atom-bound]</a>, $$\label{dir:atomic:endpoint-error}
 \|Z^{\rm end}\|_{L^2(X)}\le b_0.$$ Here $\|V\|_{L^2(X)}=(\mathbb E\|V\|_X^2)^{1/2}$; contractive projections and conditional expectations preserve upper bounds in this norm, by Jensen’s inequality.

Every other segment meeting a layer in this interval begins in $S_{p-1}$ and ends outside $S_q$: heredity puts its first endpoint in $S_q$, and the absence of either endpoint in $S_q\setminus S_{p-1}$ then forces these two alternatives. Call such segments crossing segments and write $Z^{\rm cr}$ for their sum. On every relevant layer only $Z^{\rm end}$ and $Z^{\rm cr}$ contribute.

For $p\le i\le q+1$ let $A_i$ be the minimal vertices of $S_k\setminus S_{i-1}$, and put $A_i'=A_i\cap S_i$ for $i\le q$. These are finite antichains. Each crossing segment meets every $A_i$ in exactly one vertex, since it begins in $S_{p-1}$ and ends outside $S_q$. Label it by its vertex $r\in A_p$. Disjointness allows at most one crossing segment with a given label. Let $W_r$ be its coefficient, and $d_i^r$ its vertex in $A_i$; in the absence of such a segment put $W_r=0$ and $d_i^r=\dagger$ for every $i$.

<a id="dir:atomic:crossing-energy"></a>
A later location determines an earlier one by taking its unique $A_i$-ancestor, with $\dagger$ mapping to $\dagger$. For each fixed $r$, the sigma-algebras $$\mathcal G_{i-1}^r=\mathcal F_{i-1}\vee\sigma(d_i^r)
 \qquad(p\le i\le q+1)$$ therefore increase with $i$. Define $H_{i-1}^r=\mathbb E[W_r\mid
\mathcal G_{i-1}^r]$. Scalar conditional expectation is orthogonal projection in $L^2$, whence $$\label{dir:atomic:crossing-energy}
 \sum_{i=p}^q h_i^2\le1,\qquad
 h_i^2:=\sum_{r\in A_p}\mathbb E|H_i^r-H_{i-1}^r|^2.$$ Indeed the sum for each $r$ telescopes to at most $\mathbb E W_r^2$, and the sum of those quantities is at most one. Different labels may use different sigma-algebras; no joint refinement across labels is being asserted.

Let $J_i^r$ be the part of the crossing segment with label $r$ in $B_i$, empty if the label is absent. It is determined by $d_{i+1}^r$: the previous location is recovered from it, and the portion runs from $d_i^r$ up to but excluding $d_{i+1}^r$. If these locations agree the portion is empty. Therefore $$E_i\sum_r W_r s_{J_i^r}^*=E_i\sum_r H_i^r s_{J_i^r}^*.$$ Replacing $H_i^r$ by $H_{i-1}^r$ in this expression costs at most $h_i$ in $L^2(X)$, by disjointness, <a href="#dir:atomic:atom-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atom-bound">[dir:atomic:atom-bound]</a>, and conditional Jensen. Together with <a href="#dir:atomic:endpoint-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:endpoint-error">[dir:atomic:endpoint-error]</a>, this reduces the layer estimate to bounding $$V_i=E_i\sum_r H_{i-1}^r s_{J_i^r}^*.$$

<a id="dir:atomic:endpoint-final"></a>
Fix a history through time $i-1$. For $v\in A_i'$, let $r(v)$ be its unique $A_p$-ancestor and let $m(v)$ be the mean of $W_{r(v)}$ conditional on this history and $d_i^{r(v)}=v$. Set it to zero on an empty conditional event. Write $$p_j(v)=\Pr(d_i^{r(v)}=v\mid\mathcal F_j),\qquad j=i-1,i.$$ On either child history, $p_i(v)\le2p_{i-1}(v)$, because that history has half its parent’s probability. The contribution from $v$ to $V_i$ is $m(v)p_i(v)$ times an average of segment functionals starting at $v$. The descendant subtrees at different $v$ are disjoint. Independently sampling one segment from each of these averages and applying <a href="#dir:atomic:atom-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atom-bound">[dir:atomic:atom-bound]</a> before averaging proves $$\|V_i\|_X\le
 \left(\sum_{v\in A_i'}|m(v)p_i(v)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le2\left(\sum_{v\in A_i'}|m(v)p_{i-1}(v)|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ The products in the final sum are precisely the coordinates of $E_{i-1}Z^{\rm cr}$ on $A_i'$. The antichain norm, the parent-tail bound in <a href="#dir:atomic:endpoint-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:endpoint-layer">[dir:atomic:endpoint-layer]</a>, and <a href="#dir:atomic:endpoint-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:endpoint-error">[dir:atomic:endpoint-error]</a> now give $$\|V_i\|_{L^2(X)}\le2(\eta+b_0).$$ Combining the three contributions yields, for every $p\le i\le q$, $$\label{dir:atomic:endpoint-final}
 c\le2\eta+3\sqrt{2/M}+h_i.$$ By <a href="#dir:atomic:crossing-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:crossing-energy">[dir:atomic:crossing-energy]</a>, some $h_i\le L^{-1/2}$. Since $2\eta=c/8<c$, choose $M$ and then $L$ so that $3\sqrt{2/M}+L^{-1/2}<c-2\eta$. This contradicts <a href="#dir:atomic:endpoint-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:endpoint-final">[dir:atomic:endpoint-final]</a> and proves Theorem <a href="#dir:atomic:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:obstruction">8</a>.

<a id="dir:atomic:status-route"></a>
#### Start intervals and terminating segments

A second proof uses a smaller counting budget, one charge per segment, and a different prediction space. It allows old segments to terminate inside the chosen interval. Recording their last vertices is the additional information needed to retain a refining sequence of partitions.

<a id="dir:atomic:status-constants"></a>
<a id="dir:atomic:status-layer"></a>
Assume again that <a href="#dir:atomic:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:normalization">[dir:atomic:normalization]</a> is available for all $k$ at a fixed $D$. This time choose $$\label{dir:atomic:status-constants}
 c_0=\frac1{2D},\qquad \delta=\frac{c_0}{2},\qquad
 \eta>0,\quad3\eta<\frac{c_0}{2},\quad4\eta<\frac\delta4.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#dir:atomic:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:preparation">9</a>, put $H_i=S_i$ and $J_i=H_{i+1}\setminus H_i$, and write $$Z=\sum_{I\in\mathcal I}\lambda_I s_I^*,\qquad
 \sum_I\lambda_I^2\le1,\qquad U_i=E_iZ=P_{S_k}M_i.$$ The common preparation gives the exact conditional-mean identity. Since $1/D=2c_0$ and $\eta<c_0$, its bounds imply <a id="dir:atomic:status-selected"></a>$$\label{dir:atomic:status-selected}
 \|Q_{H_i}M_i\|<\eta,\qquad
 \|P_{J_i}M_{i+1}\|\ge2c_0-4\eta,$$ and, in particular, the weaker bounds $$\label{dir:atomic:status-layer}
 \|Q_{H_i}U_i\|\le2\eta,\qquad
 \|P_{J_i}U_{i+1}\|\ge c_0-3\eta>\delta.$$ We keep this slack in the estimates below. The distinction from the endpoint argument will be that segments may terminate inside the chosen interval; their last vertices must therefore remain in the prediction information.

Take $k=NL$ and divide its steps into intervals $r\le i<r+L$, where $r$ ranges over $0,L,\ldots,(N-1)L$. For each interval put $$B_r=\sum_{\min I\in H_{r+L}\setminus H_r}\lambda_I^2,
 \qquad b_r=\mathbb E B_r.$$ Every segment has one first vertex, so $\sum_r b_r\le1$. Fix one such interval. Call a sampled segment old if its first vertex lies in $H_r$, and new if its first vertex lies in $H_{r+L}\setminus H_r$. No other segment meets a layer $J_i$ of this interval, by heredity. The new contribution after projection and conditional expectation has $L^2(X)$ norm at most $\sqrt{b_r}$, because its pointwise norm before conditioning is at most $\sqrt{B_r}$.

To predict the coefficients of old segments, use the finite set of pairs $(\omega,I)$ with $\omega$ a terminal sample outcome and $I$ an old segment in its sampled atom. Give each such pair mass $\Pr(\omega)$, obtaining a finite measure $\mu$, and write $\lambda(\omega,I)=\lambda_I(\omega)$. Then $$\int\lambda^2\,d\mu
 =\mathbb E\sum_{I\text{ old}}\lambda_I^2\le1.$$ If there are no pairs, all old contributions and all energies below are zero. Otherwise, for $r\le i\le r+L$, partition this pair set by the binary history through time $i$ and the following tagged status:

- if $I$ has a vertex outside $H_i$, record “active” and its first vertex outside $H_i$;

- if $I\subset H_i$, record “inactive” and its last vertex.

These partitions refine. An old segment starts in $H_r\subset H_i$, so a later active entry determines its earlier entry by the ancestor chain. A later inactive endpoint determines whether the earlier status was inactive at that endpoint or active at the first ancestor outside $H_i$. The tags distinguish these cases.

<a id="dir:atomic:status-energy"></a>
Let $m_i$ be the mean of $\lambda$ on each positive-mass cell of the $i$th partition, with arbitrary value zero on empty cells, and define $$\label{dir:atomic:status-energy}
 g_i=\int|m_{i+1}-m_i|^2\,d\mu.
 \qquad
 \sum_{i=r}^{r+L-1}g_i\le\int\lambda^2\,d\mu\le1.$$ The inequality is the orthogonality identity for conditional means in $L^2(\mu)$; it does not require $\mu$ to be a probability measure.

The next status determines $I\cap J_i$. If it is active, this portion ends just before the next entry; if it is inactive, it ends at the recorded last vertex. Its first vertex, when the portion is nonempty, is the first ancestor outside $H_i$. Equal successive entries, or an endpoint already in $H_i$, give an empty portion. Thus the clipped segment functional is constant on every cell of the $(i+1)$st partition, and $$E_{i+1}\sum_{I\text{ old}}\lambda_I P_{J_i}s_I^*
 =E_{i+1}\sum_{I\text{ old}}m_{i+1}(\omega,I)P_{J_i}s_I^*.$$ Split $m_{i+1}=(m_{i+1}-m_i)+m_i$. Disjointness of the clipped segments and conditional Jensen bound the first term in $L^2(X)$ by $\sqrt{g_i}$.

<a id="dir:atomic:status-antichain"></a>
We now bound the second term using the small parent tail. At a fixed history $h$ through time $i$, possible active entries form the antichain of minimal vertices outside $H_i$. Only finitely many occur in the finite sample space. Let $V$ be those lying in $H_{i+1}$, let $p_v$ be the conditional expected number of old segments with entry $v$, and let $m_v$ be their mean coefficient under $\mu$ conditional on $h$ and this entry. Empty cells give $m_v=0$. There is at most one such segment per outcome, by disjointness. Hence the coordinates of the conditional old contribution on $V$ are exactly $p_vm_v$. Its only other contributions in $U_i$ come from new segments. The antichain norm and <a href="#dir:atomic:status-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:status-layer">[dir:atomic:status-layer]</a> therefore imply $$\label{dir:atomic:status-antichain}
 \left(\sum_{v\in V}|p_vm_v|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le2\eta+(E_iB_r)^{1/2}.$$ For the new term, this follows from its disjoint-segment bound and conditional Cauchy–Schwarz before restricting to $V$.

At a child history $h'$ of $h$, the conditional expected number of old segments with a given previous entry $v$ is at most $2p_v$. To see the measure normalization explicitly, if $C(h,v)$ denotes the corresponding cell of pairs, then $$p_v=\frac{\mu(C(h,v))}{\Pr(h)},\qquad
 \frac{\mu(C(h,v)\cap\{\text{history }h'\})}{\Pr(h')}
 \le2p_v,$$ since $\Pr(h')=\Pr(h)/2$. This fairness assertion concerns the original binary probability, not the pair measure $\mu$.

<a id="dir:atomic:status-final"></a>
The contribution with coefficient $m_i$ at $h'$ is consequently, for each $v\in V$, $m_v$ times a nonnegative mixture of segment functionals starting at $v$, of total mass at most $2p_v$. Add a zero functional to make this mass exactly $2p_v$, when needed. Choose independently one term per $v$ from the normalized mixtures. Distinct $v$ lie in disjoint descendant subtrees, so <a href="#dir:atomic:atom-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:atom-bound">[dir:atomic:atom-bound]</a> bounds every such sum by $2(\sum_v|p_vm_v|^2)^{1/2}$. Averaging and then applying <a href="#dir:atomic:status-antichain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:status-antichain">[dir:atomic:status-antichain]</a> gives an $L^2(X)$ bound $4\eta+2\sqrt{b_r}$ for this term. Adding the prediction difference and the new contribution proves $$\label{dir:atomic:status-final}
 \delta\le4\eta+3\sqrt{b_r}+\sqrt{g_i}
 \qquad(r\le i<r+L).$$

Choose $L>4/\delta^2$ first. Then choose $N$ large enough that $3/\sqrt N<\delta/4$, and take $k=NL$. Some interval satisfies $b_r\le1/N$, since $\sum_r b_r\le1$. For this interval, <a href="#dir:atomic:status-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:status-constants">[dir:atomic:status-constants]</a> and <a href="#dir:atomic:status-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:status-final">[dir:atomic:status-final]</a> force $\sqrt{g_i}>\delta/2$ at every step. This contradicts <a href="#dir:atomic:status-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:atomic:status-energy">[dir:atomic:status-energy]</a>, because $$\sum_{i=r}^{r+L-1}g_i>L\delta^2/4>1.$$ This completes the second proof of Theorem <a href="#dir:atomic:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:atomic:obstruction">8</a>.

The two estimates retain different information. In the endpoint proof, the selected interval has endpoint energy at most $2/M$, all surviving segments cross the entire interval, and the coefficient predictions are scalar martingales for each boundary label. In the start proof, total start energy is at most one, segments may terminate inside the interval, and prediction uses their refining active or inactive statuses on a finite measure space. These are direct obstruction arguments with their respective parameter choices; neither calculation above asserts an asymptotic midpoint modulus.

<a id="dir:layers"></a>
### Fresh coordinate layers along diamond slopes

We now use the linear path-cost space. Its intrinsic estimates control either a fresh part of a finite martingale or the squared-norm increase of a symmetric split. The graph arguments below supply precisely those inputs, with their separate support and measurability conditions.

For $T_n=\mathbb N^{\le n}$, including its root, let $b_t=\sum_{s\preceq t}e_s$ and $$L_n(z)=\inf_{z=h+\sum_t\alpha_tb_t}
          \bigl(\|h\|_{\ell_2(T_n)}+\|\alpha\|_{\ell_1(T_n)}\bigr),
 \qquad
 X_{\rm lin}=\left(\bigoplus_{n\ge1}(\ell_2(T_n),L_n)\right)_2.$$ The infimum ranges over real $h\in\ell_2(T_n)$ and $\alpha\in\ell_1(T_n)$; it need not be attained. The intrinsic estimates below allow arbitrarily close decompositions. This definition is the linear path-cost model of (OpenAI 2026a, Propositions 5.1 and 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)]. In particular, that paper proves the equivalence with the bounded global-test norm on each finite-height component. No infinite-height completion is substituted for this Hilbert sum.

For a finite ancestral set $A$ in the forest, $P_A$ is contractive on $X_{\rm lin}$: it restricts a Hilbert vector and truncates every $b_t$ to another root path or zero. Finite coordinate vectors are dense because $\|z\|_2/\sqrt{n+1}\le L_n(z)\le\|z\|_2$ on each component. Thus the $P_A$ approximate the identity strongly. For $A\subseteq B$, the layer projection is $P_{B\setminus A}=P_B-P_A$ and has norm at most two. The tail projection $I-P_A$ also has norm at most two.

We use three forms of coordinate selection. Exact graph slopes can be paired with windows chosen at the current history, or with one head fixed across a whole level. Estimates requiring exact support instead use finite approximations: the approximating slopes retain the exact parent–child averaging identity, and the next support is fixed before the next sign. We give each intrinsic estimate immediately before the construction that meets its hypotheses.

Throughout, the probability space is a uniform finite sign cube, $\mathcal F_i$ records its first $i$ signs, and a dyadic martingale satisfies $W_i=\mathbb E[W_m\mid\mathcal F_i]$. The estimates are the separate sign-prediction, variation, and stopping arguments proved in (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)]; no constant is transferred between these proofs.

<a id="dir:adaptive-windows"></a>
#### Exact slopes with adaptive windows

<a id="dir:sign-input"></a>
The weighted sign-prediction estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] supplies an absolute constant $C_{\rm sign}$ such that, for finite ancestral windows $A_i\subseteq B_i$ measurable with respect to $\mathcal F_i$, with $B_i\subseteq A_{i+1}$ whenever $i+1<m$, $$\label{dir:sign-input}
 \sum_{i<m}\mathbb E\|P_{B_i\setminus A_i}W_{i+1}\|^2
 \le C_{\rm sign}\left(\mathbb E\|W_m\|^2+
       \sum_{i<m}\mathbb E\|P_{B_i\setminus A_i}W_i\|^2\right).$$ There is no support assumption on $W_i$ and no specified numerical value for $C_{\rm sign}$.

Suppose $f:\mathcal D_k\to X_{\rm lin}$ is noncontracting and $D$-Lipschitz, where $k\ge1$. We recursively select a middle vertex at each encountered edge, then choose a half-edge by a fair sign. The resulting true slopes $W_i$ form a dyadic martingale and satisfy $\|W_i\|\le D$. At a history of length $i$, choose a finite ancestral $A_i$, containing $B_{i-1}$ when $i\ge1$, with $\|(I-P_{A_i})W_i\|\le2^{-k}$. For each middle vertex consider its two child slopes. The first-child sequence is pairwise $2$-separated, as is the second-child sequence. Each sequence has only finitely many members with tail norm at most $1/2$: infinitely many such members, together with compactness of their bounded head projections, would give a pair at distance less than $2$. Thus one middle vertex has *both* child tails greater than $1/2$.

Before tossing the next sign, enlarge $A_i$ to a finite ancestral $B_i$ capturing these two child tails closely enough that $$\|P_{B_i\setminus A_i}W_{i+1}\|>1/4$$ on either outcome. For the parent, $P_{B_i\setminus A_i}W_i=P_{B_i}(I-P_{A_i})W_i$, so its layer norm is at most $2^{-k}$. All windows are known before the next sign and are disjoint along each complete history. Applying <a href="#dir:sign-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:sign-input">[dir:sign-input]</a> therefore gives <a id="dir:adaptive-conclusion"></a>$$\label{dir:adaptive-conclusion}
 \frac{k}{16}\le C_{\rm sign}(D^2+k2^{-2k})
 \le C_{\rm sign}(D^2+1).$$ The constant remains independent of $k$ and $D$.

<a id="dir:deterministic-heads"></a>
#### Exact slopes with a common head at each level

<a id="dir:leakage-input"></a>
For fixed finite ancestral $A_0\subseteq\cdots\subseteq A_m$, with $E_i=A_i\setminus A_{i-1}$, the deterministic-head leakage estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 6.5) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] is $$\label{dir:leakage-input}
 \sum_{i=1}^m\mathbb E\|P_{E_i}W_i\|^2
 \le1024\left(\mathbb E\|W_m\|^2+
                 \sum_{i=1}^m\mathbb E\|P_{E_i}W_{i-1}\|^2\right).$$ It imposes no support restriction on the martingale.

Return to the noncontracting normalization and keep the true slopes $W_i$, so $\|W_i\|\le D$. Put $\varepsilon=\min\{1/8,1/\sqrt{k}\}$. Once the finitely many slopes at level $i$ have been selected, choose a single finite ancestral $A_i$ containing $A_{i-1}$ when $i\ge1$ and satisfying $$\|(I-P_{A_i})W_i\|\le\varepsilon$$ for every history at that level. At each current edge the first-child slopes are pairwise $2$-separated. Two have head projections less than $1/2$ apart; one therefore has tail norm at least $3/4$. Keep that middle vertex and its two half-edges as the next fair choice. After all choices at this level, enlarge the common head to approximate all next slopes as well.

For $E_{i+1}=A_{i+1}\setminus A_i$, the first-child outcome satisfies $\|P_{E_{i+1}}W_{i+1}\|\ge3/4-\varepsilon\ge1/2$. This outcome has conditional probability one half. On all outcomes the parent layer norm is at most $\varepsilon$, because $P_{E_{i+1}}W_i=P_{A_{i+1}}(I-P_{A_i})W_i$. The sets $A_i$ are fixed across each level, exactly as required by <a href="#dir:leakage-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:leakage-input">[dir:leakage-input]</a>. It follows that <a id="dir:leakage-conclusion"></a>$$\label{dir:leakage-conclusion}
 \frac{k}{8}\le1024(D^2+k\varepsilon^2)
 \le1024(D^2+1).$$

<a id="dir:predictable-application"></a>
#### Supported approximations with predictable heads

<a id="dir:predictable-input"></a>
The predictable-layer estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] applies when $P_0$ is a fixed finite ancestral set, $P_i$ is finite ancestral and $\mathcal F_{i-1}$-measurable for $i\ge1$, $P_{i-1}\subseteq P_i$, and $W_i=P_{P_i}W_i$. If $\|W_m\|\le C$ on every outcome, it gives $$\label{dir:predictable-input}
 \sum_{i=1}^m\mathbb E\|P_{P_i\setminus P_{i-1}}W_i\|^2\le495C^2.$$

For the estimate with constant $495$, keep the noncontracting normalization. Set $\alpha=1/[8(k+1)]$. Approximate the initial true slope within $\alpha$ by a finite vector $W_0$ supported on a finite ancestral $P_0$. At a history of length $i-1$, choose from the true edge a deviation $z$ with $$\|(I-P_{P_{i-1}})z\|\ge3/4.$$ To do so, take two of the $2$-separated deviations with projected difference less than $1/2$, and use the triangle inequality for their tails. Approximate $z$ within $\alpha$ by a finite vector $v_i$ and choose a finite ancestral $P_i\supseteq P_{i-1}$ containing its support. For the next fair sign put $W_i=W_{i-1}\pm v_i$ and follow the corresponding actual half-edge.

The sets $P_i$ and vectors $v_i$ are fixed before this sign. Consequently the $W_i$ form an exact dyadic martingale, are supported in $P_i$, and differ from their true slopes by less than $(i+1)\alpha$. In particular, $\|W_k\|\le D+1$. Since the parent has no coordinates outside $P_{i-1}$, on either sign $$\|P_{P_i\setminus P_{i-1}}W_i\|
 =\|(I-P_{P_{i-1}})v_i\|
 \ge3/4-2\alpha\ge1/2.$$ The factor two uses only the bound for $I-P_{P_{i-1}}$. Every hypothesis of <a href="#dir:predictable-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:predictable-input">[dir:predictable-input]</a> now holds, giving <a id="dir:495-conclusion"></a>$$\label{dir:495-conclusion}
 \frac{k}{4}\le495(D+1)^2.$$

<a id="dir:variation-input"></a>
Under the same predictable support hypotheses, the separate variation-and-mass-removal estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 6.3) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] gives $$\label{dir:variation-input}
 \sum_{i=1}^m\mathbb E\|P_{P_i\setminus P_{i-1}}W_i\|^2\le252C^2.$$ In particular its bound is $1008$ when $C=2$.

The variation estimate has a useful different graph normalization. Rescale the graph to upper constant one and lower constant $1/D$, and put $\varepsilon=\min\{1/(4D),1/(k+1)\}$. Approximate the initial slope within $\varepsilon$ as before. Given a finite head space $E=\operatorname{ran}P_{P_{i-1}}$, some deviation has distance from $E$ greater than $1/(2D)$. Otherwise all deviations would have bounded approximants in $E$ with errors less than $3/(4D)$, and two close approximants would contradict their separation $2/D$. Choose this deviation, approximate it by a finite $v_i$ with error less than $\varepsilon$, and update $W_i=W_{i-1}\pm v_i$ using a predictable ancestral enlargement $P_i$. The cumulative error is less than $(i+1)\varepsilon$, and hence $\|W_k\|\le2$. Moreover, $$\|P_{P_i\setminus P_{i-1}}W_i\|
 =\|v_i-P_{P_{i-1}}v_i\|
 \ge\operatorname{dist}(v_i,E)>\frac1{4D}.$$ Using <a href="#dir:variation-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:variation-input">[dir:variation-input]</a> with $C=2$ proves <a id="dir:1008-conclusion"></a>$$\label{dir:1008-conclusion}
 \frac{k}{16D^2}\le1008.$$ The distance-to-head argument avoids requiring the tail projection to be contractive.

<a id="dir:gap-application"></a>
