#### A telescoping squared-gap argument

<a id="dir:gap-input"></a>
For finite ancestral $A\subseteq A'$, $x=P_Ax$, and $y=P_{A'}y$, the fresh-layer squared-gap estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.11) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] is $$\label{dir:gap-input}
 \|P_{A'\setminus A}y\|^2\le
 400\left(\frac{\|x+y\|^2+\|x-y\|^2}{2}-\|x\|^2\right).$$ The increment $y$ may have a nonzero part in $A$. Averaging a convexity gain and telescoping is the martingale method for uniformly convex norms (Pisier 2011, Theorem 4.6 and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Pisier2011](#ref-Pisier2011)]. Here the estimate controls only the part of the increment in the new coordinate layer.

Normalize the graph to upper constant one and lower constant $1/D$. Let $a=2/D$ and choose $0<\gamma<\min\{a/8,1/(k+1)\}$. At the root use a finite ancestral projection $W_0=P_{A_0}x_0$ of the true slope, with error at most $\gamma$. Work successively by levels. Given a current true slope and $A_{i-1}$, its $a$-separated deviations have one member $z$ with $\|(I-P_{A_{i-1}})z\|\ge a/4$, by the same finite-head compactness argument. Choose one at each of the finitely many histories. Enlarge to a common finite ancestral $A_i$ so that $\|z-P_{A_i}z\|\le\gamma$ for all these choices. Set $v_i=P_{A_i}z$ and define $W_i=W_{i-1}\pm v_i$, associated with the actual half-edges. The parent is supported in $A_{i-1}$, the increment is supported in $A_i$, and every approximation error is at most $(i+1)\gamma$. Thus $\|W_i\|\le2$.

For $E_i=A_i\setminus A_{i-1}$, nesting of the projections gives $$P_{E_i}v_i=(P_{A_i}-P_{A_{i-1}})z,
 \qquad \|P_{E_i}v_i\|\ge a/4-\gamma>a/8.$$ Here the error is exactly $z-P_{A_i}z$, whose norm is at most $\gamma$; no layer-projection bound enters this comparison. Apply <a href="#dir:gap-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:gap-input">[dir:gap-input]</a> at every parent with $x=W_{i-1}$ and $y=v_i$. Averaging over a level and then summing the squared-norm increases yields <a id="dir:400-conclusion"></a>$$\label{dir:400-conclusion}
 4\ge\mathbb E\|W_k\|^2
 \ge\|W_0\|^2+\frac{k(a/8)^2}{400}
 \ge\frac{k(a/8)^2}{400}.$$ Thus this direct argument also excludes a uniform distortion bound. Only a finite binary tree was averaged after the coordinate choices; the availability of infinitely many middle vertices was used at each selection step.

<a id="met:transfer-section"></a>
### Midpoint transfer rules

The principal forest bound used an arbitrary-displacement quadratic estimate. We now give transfers for other intrinsic inputs, to be used in the path-cost and recursive models below. The three mechanisms are additive loss of a power of the slope, exclusion of separated lenses, and finite-codimensional gain. The approximation head is fixed before selecting a pair of deviations; no child supremum is assumed attained.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Homogeneous tail estimates and separated lenses

<a id="stmt-met-tail-to-lens"></a>
<div id="met:tail-to-lens" class="proposition">

<a id="met:radial-input"></a>
<a id="met:square-input"></a>
<a id="met:radial-lens"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Homogeneous tail estimates). *Let $X$ have a specified coordinate system with dense finite-support vectors. Fix a family $\mathcal P$ of bounded finite-rank coordinate projections such that every finite support is contained in the range of some $P\in\mathcal P$. Consider separately the following two hypotheses, each imposed for every $P\in\mathcal P$, all pure head and tail vectors $p=Pp$, $Py=0$, and every $R>0$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \|y\|^2&\le C^2 R(R-\|p\|)
 &&\text{if }\|p\pm y\|\le R, \label{met:radial-input}\\
 \|p\|^2+a\|y\|^2&\le R^2
 &&\text{if }\|p\pm y\|\le R. \label{met:square-input}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $C>0$, respectively $a>0$, is uniform over $\mathcal P$, the vectors, and $R$. No uniform bound on $\|P\|$ or $\|I-P\|$ is assumed. For $R>0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, an infinite $\varepsilon$-separated family in the symmetric $R$-lens at $x$ then satisfies, respectively, <a id="met:square-lens"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \varepsilon^2/4&\le C^2R(R-\|x\|),\label{met:radial-lens}\\
 \|x\|^2+a\varepsilon^2/4&\le R^2.\label{met:square-lens}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-tail-to-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-tail-to-lens</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $p$ and $P$ as in Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>, and apply the assumed estimate to $p,y$ at radius $R+2\eta$. In the first case, $$(\varepsilon/2-\eta)^2
 \le C^2(R+2\eta)(R+2\eta-\|p\|)
 \le C^2(R+2\eta)(R+3\eta-\|x\|).$$ In the second case, $\max\{\|x\|-\eta,0\}^2+a(\varepsilon/2-\eta)^2\le(R+2\eta)^2$. Take $0<\eta<\varepsilon/2$ and let $\eta$ decrease to zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:squared-section"></a>
#### Squared losses and additive losses

For the noncontracting normalization define $$R_j=\sup\{\|x_e\|: e\text{ is a stage-$j$ edge}\},
 \qquad 1\le R_j\le D.$$ The countable supremum need not be attained. Our estimates apply to each parent edge before the supremum is taken.

The squared-lens hypothesis below is the $p=2$ Banach-space form of the metric diamond inequality in (Malthaner 2025, Equation (2) and Theorem 3.7 of arXiv v2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Malthaner2025](#ref-Malthaner2025)]: its endpoints are $-x,x$ and its intermediate points are the deviations $z_i$. We retain the coefficient by summing the squared losses directly.

<a id="stmt-met-squared-transfer"></a>
<div id="met:squared-transfer" class="theorem">

<a id="met:additive-bound"></a>
**Theorem 11**. *If every infinite $\varepsilon$-separated symmetric $R$-lens satisfies $\|x\|^2\le R^2-b\varepsilon^2$ for some $b>0$, then every embedding <a href="#met:all-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:all-pairs">[met:all-pairs]</a> satisfies $D^2\ge1+4bk$. If instead <a href="#met:radial-lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:radial-lens">[met:radial-lens]</a> holds, then $$\label{met:additive-bound}
 k\le C^2D(D-1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-squared-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-squared-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* For each stage-$j$ parent, its children lie in the lens of radius $R_{j+1}$ and its deviations are $2$-separated. The first hypothesis gives $\|x\|^2\le R_{j+1}^2-4b$. Taking suprema gives $R_j^2\le R_{j+1}^2-4b$, and summing yields $1\le R_0^2\le D^2-4bk$. For the radial estimate, $$1\le C^2R_{j+1}(R_{j+1}-\|x\|),\qquad
 R_j\le R_{j+1}-\frac1{C^2R_{j+1}}
 \le R_{j+1}-\frac1{C^2D}.$$ Summing proves <a href="#met:additive-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:additive-bound">[met:additive-bound]</a>. Empty sums cover $k=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Retaining a homogeneous power loss

The radius dependence of a higher-power input can be accumulated directly. Replacing it by a fixed contraction factor would discard this information.

<a id="stmt-met-power-transfer"></a>
<div id="met:power-transfer" class="theorem">

**Theorem 12**. *Assume the density and projection-family hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a>. Let $q\ge2$ and $c>0$. Suppose that for every admissible projection, every pure head–tail pair $p,y$, and every $R>0$, $$\|p\pm y\|\le R
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|p\|^2+cR^{2-q}\|y\|^q\le R^2.$$ Then every distortion-$D$ embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ satisfies $$ck\le D^{q-2}(D^2-1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-power-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-power-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Use the noncontracting normalization. Fix a parent $x$ whose child slopes have norm at most $R$. Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>, followed by $\eta\downarrow0$, gives $$\|x\|^2+cR^{2-q}\le R^2.$$ Here the paired tail has limiting lower norm one. Set $R=R_{j+1}$ and take the supremum over stage-$j$ parents. Since $1\le R_{j+1}\le D$ and $q\ge2$, $$R_{j+1}^2-R_j^2\ge cR_{j+1}^{2-q}\ge cD^{2-q}.$$ Summing between $R_0\ge1$ and $R_k\le D$ gives $D^2-1\ge kcD^{2-q}$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:parametric-section"></a>
#### Keeping the parameterized test losses

Some primal tests give a linear gain before optimization. The graph argument can preserve that parameter instead of replacing the input by a single modulus.

<a id="stmt-met-parametric"></a>
<div id="met:parametric" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *Assume the density and projection-family hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a>. Fix constants $a,b>0$. Suppose that for every admissible projection and every pure head–tail pair $p,y$, the following estimate holds for all $\lambda>0$: $$\max_\pm\|p\pm y\|\ge
 \frac{\|p\|+a\lambda\|y\|}{\sqrt{1+b\lambda^2}}.$$ Put $Q=\sqrt{1+b\lambda^2}$. For noncontracting diamond slopes bounded above by $D$, the stage suprema satisfy $$R_j\le Q R_{j+1}-a\lambda
 \le R_{j+1}-\bigl(a\lambda-(Q-1)D\bigr).$$ In particular, whenever $\Delta=a\lambda-(Q-1)D>0$, one has $1+k\Delta\le D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-parametric"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-parametric</summary>

*Proof.* For each parent $x$, choose a finite-support approximation $p$ and a paired tail $y$ as in Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>. The half-difference has norm at least one, so $\|y\|\ge1-\eta$. If the child bound is $R$, then $R+2\eta\ge(\|p\|+a\lambda(1-\eta))/Q$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$ to obtain $\|x\|\le QR-a\lambda$, and then take suprema. Summation proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:backward-section"></a>
#### Backward contraction

An exact power-law lens estimate is useful but unnecessary for a qualitative obstruction. The following form also handles estimates whose constants are selected only after the separation is specified.

<a id="stmt-met-backward"></a>
<div id="met:backward" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *Fix $0<\alpha\le1$. Suppose some $0\le\rho<1$ has the following property: whenever $\|x\pm z_i\|\le1$ and $\|z_i-z_j\|\ge2\alpha$ for $i\ne j$, one has $\|x\|\le\rho$. An embedding with lower factor $\alpha$ and upper factor one must satisfy <a id="met:backward-bound"></a>$$\label{met:backward-bound}
 \alpha\le\rho^k,$$ where the empty product $\rho^0$ is one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-backward"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-backward</summary>

*Proof.* Let $r_j$ be the supremum of stage-$j$ slope norms. Initially $r_k\le1$, and every $r_j\ge\alpha>0$. If all child slopes are at most $r_{j+1}$, normalize <a href="#met:slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:slopes">[met:slopes]</a> by this positive number. The resulting unit lens has separation at least $2\alpha/r_{j+1}\ge2\alpha$. The hypothesis gives $\|x\|\le\rho r_{j+1}$ for every parent, hence $r_j\le\rho r_{j+1}$. Induction proves the assertion. If $\rho=0$, the existence of any replacement already contradicts $r_j\ge\alpha$. ◻

</details>

For example, <a href="#met:radial-lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:radial-lens">[met:radial-lens]</a> gives $\rho=1-\alpha^2/C^2$ when this number is positive. If it is nonpositive, a parent lens with center norm at least $\alpha>0$ is already impossible. With upper factor one it also gives the stronger intermediate inequality $$\|x\|\le r-\frac{\alpha^2}{C^2r}
 \le r-\frac{\alpha^2}{C^2},\qquad 0<r\le1.$$ Thus $k\le C^2(D^2-D)$ when $\alpha=1/D$. Alternatively, if the proved lens input is $\|x\|\le1-\eta(2\alpha)$, the direct conclusion is $\alpha\le(1-\eta(2\alpha))^k$; a polynomial rate requires an actual quantitative bound for $\eta$.

<a id="met:selection-section"></a>
#### Enlarged lenses and stable parents

<span id="met:bridge:enlarged-section" label="met:bridge:enlarged-section"></span> A unit-ball lens gap bounds the parent relative to the child bound. The equivalent normalization below divides by the parent norm instead. It also identifies an actual parent at which all children have moderate stretch.

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-enlarged-backward"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:enlarged-backward" class="proposition">

<a id="met:bridge:enlarged-depth"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Enlarged lenses and a stable parent). *<span id="met:stable-parent" label="met:stable-parent"></span>Let $D\ge1$ and $\delta>0$. If $(1+\delta)^k>D$, every noncontracting embedding with upper factor $D$ has a parent edge all of whose child slopes have norm at most $(1+\delta)$ times its own slope norm. If, for every unit $u$, the enlarged lens $$M(u,\delta)=\{z:\max_\pm\|u\pm z\|\le1+\delta\}$$ contains no infinite $2/D$-separated family, then every such embedding satisfies $$\label{met:bridge:enlarged-depth}
 1\le \frac{D}{(1+\delta)^k}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-enlarged-backward"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-enlarged-backward</summary>

*Proof.* If no stable parent existed, choose at each stage an actual child whose slope norm is greater than $1+\delta$ times its parent’s norm. Starting with norm at least one would contradict the terminal upper bound $D$. At a stable parent of norm $r\le D$, division by $r$ puts all deviations in $M(x/r,\delta)$ with separation at least $2/r\ge2/D$. This proves the second assertion by contradiction.

The same exclusion also gives a backward proof. If all children of a parent of norm $r$ have norm at most $L\le D$, then $r\ge L/(1+\delta)$ would put the normalized deviations in the forbidden lens. Thus every parent has norm less than $L/(1+\delta)$; taking suprema gives the non-strict bound, and iteration from $L=D$ proves <a href="#met:bridge:enlarged-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:enlarged-depth">[met:bridge:enlarged-depth]</a>. Neither proof assumes attainment of a child supremum. ◻

</details>

The stable parent may instead be divided by its child bound $(1+\delta)r$. Its unit-ball lens has center norm $1/(1+\delta)$ and separation at least $2/((1+\delta)D)$. This is useful when the intrinsic estimate is stated for unit-ball lenses rather than enlarged unit-center lenses.

<a id="met:packing-section"></a>
#### Uniform finite-head bounds and their parameters

Many estimates above control all lens vectors using one head selected from the center. This order of quantifiers is precisely what packing requires.

<a id="stmt-met-packing"></a>
<div id="met:packing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *Let $P$ be bounded and finite rank, let $A\subset X$ be bounded, and let $b\ge0$ and $\varepsilon>0$. If $\|(I-P)v\|\le b$ for all $v\in A$, then $A$ contains no infinite $\varepsilon$-separated family when $2b<\varepsilon$. Consequently the following two inputs have different graph conclusions.*

1.  *Let $0<\delta<1$. For each $\|x\|>1-\delta$, suppose one such $P$ bounds by $b(\delta)$ the tails of every $v$ with $\|x\pm v\|\le1$. If $2b(\delta)<2/D$, backward contraction gives $D^{-1}\le(1-\delta)^k$.*

2.  *Let $\delta>0$. For every unit $x$, suppose one such $P$ bounds by $b(\delta)$ the tails of every $v$ with $\|x\pm v\|\le1+\delta$. If $2b(\delta)<2/D$, actual-child selection gives $D\ge(1+\delta)^k$.*

*The same conclusions hold when the bound is on the first endpoint of every pair with $(u+v)/2=x$: take $u,v\in B_X$ in case (1), and $u,v\in(1+\delta)B_X$ in case (2).*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-packing</summary>

*Proof.* If $(v_i)$ were such a separated family, choose distinct $i,j$ with $\|Pv_i-Pv_j\|<\varepsilon-2b$. The triangle inequality contradicts $\|v_i-v_j\|\ge\varepsilon$. The graph conclusions follow from Propositions <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a> and <a href="#met:stable-parent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:stable-parent">[met:stable-parent]</a>. For first endpoints, translation by their common center preserves separation and the same finite-dimensional argument applies directly to $(u_i)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:g-parameters"></a>
Under the projection-family hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a>, a bound only on pure tails uses Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a> for a second form of compactness. For example, suppose unit-ball head–tail pairs obey $\|y\|\le g(1-\|p\|)$, where $g:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)$ is nondecreasing and $g(r)\to0$ as $r\downarrow0$. For $t>0$, an infinite $2t$-separated unit lens cannot have center norm greater than $1-\Delta$ if $$\label{met:g-parameters}
 0<\Delta<1/4,\qquad (1+2\Delta)g(4\Delta)+\Delta<t.$$ Indeed choose a head approximating the center within $\Delta$ and a half-difference with head norm at most $\Delta$. After division by $1+2\Delta$, its tail norm is at least $(t-\Delta)/(1+2\Delta)$, whereas the normalized center deficit is at most $4\Delta$. This contradicts <a href="#met:g-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:g-parameters">[met:g-parameters]</a>. Thus with $t=1/D$ the resulting backward factor is $1-\Delta$. Neither monotonicity nor a power-law rate is presumed for a function until its actual formula establishes it.

<a id="met:bridge:sequential-section"></a>
#### From a qualitative sequential estimate to a uniform lens gap

The qualitative argument can start with a statement about sequences of pure head–tail pairs, without first constructing a numerical modulus.

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-sequential-lens"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:sequential-lens" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *Assume the density and projection-family hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a>. Suppose that whenever admissible heads $P_n\in\mathcal P$ and vectors $p_n,y_n$ satisfy $$P_np_n=p_n,\qquad P_ny_n=0,\qquad
 \|p_n\pm y_n\|\le1,\qquad \|p_n\|\longrightarrow1,$$ one has $\|y_n\|\longrightarrow0$. The heads are allowed to vary with $n$. Then, for each $\varepsilon>0$, there is $0<\rho<1$ such that every infinite $\varepsilon$-separated symmetric unit lens has center norm at most $\rho$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-sequential-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-sequential-lens</summary>

*Proof.* If no such $\rho$ existed, choose centers $x_n$ with $\|x_n\|\to1$ and infinite $\varepsilon$-separated families $(z_{n,i})_i$ with $\|x_n\pm z_{n,i}\|\le1$. Choose $\eta_n\downarrow0$, finitely supported $p_n$ with $\|p_n-x_n\|<\eta_n$, and admissible heads $P_n$ satisfying $P_np_n=p_n$. Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a> gives a half-difference $w_n$ and the tail $y_n=w_n-P_nw_n$ with $$\|y_n\|\ge\varepsilon/2-\eta_n,\qquad
 \|p_n\|\ge\|x_n\|-\eta_n,\qquad
 \|p_n\pm y_n\|\le1+2\eta_n.$$ Divide both vectors by $1+2\eta_n$. The resulting pure head–tail pairs have endpoints in the unit ball and centers whose norms tend to one. Their tail norms have lower limit at least $\varepsilon/2>0$, contradicting the assumed sequential statement. This contradiction gives one $\rho$ valid for all centers and heads. ◻

</details>

In the upper-one graph normalization, take $\varepsilon=2/D$. Dividing a parent configuration by any child bound $0<L\le1$ leaves separation at least $2/(DL)\ge\varepsilon$. Thus the lemma gives the parent bound $\rho L$ and Proposition <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a> yields $1/D\le\rho^k$.

<a id="met:quotient-section"></a>
#### Finite-codimensional gains

The relation between finite-codimensional midpoint gains and small symmetric lenses is developed in (Dilworth et al. 2016, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. Here we retain the quantitative difference between selecting an actual child with a reserved error margin and taking a limiting supremum. The next transfer uses the symmetric maximum, so it also applies when the intrinsic theorem controls the average of the endpoint norms.

<a id="stmt-met-quotient-transfer"></a>
<div id="met:quotient-transfer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18**. *Suppose that for some $t>0$ and $g>0$, every unit vector $u\in X$ admits a closed finite-codimensional subspace $F_u$ such that $$y\in F_u,\quad\|y\|\ge t
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \max\{\|u+y\|,\|u-y\|\}\ge1+g.$$ If $D\ge1$ and $t\le1/(2D)$, every noncontracting embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ with upper factor $D$ satisfies $D\ge(1+g/2)^k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-quotient-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-quotient-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* For a parent slope $x$, put $r=\|x\|\in[1,D]$ and use the subspace $F_{x/r}$. Apply the quotient part of Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a> to choose $w=(z_i-z_j)/2$, with $\|w\|\ge1$, and $w'\in F_{x/r}$ such that $\|w-w'\|<\min\{1/2,rg/2\}$. Then $\|w'/r\|\ge1/(2D)\ge t$, and $$\max_\pm\|x\pm w\|\ge r(1+g)-\|w-w'\|>r(1+g/2).$$ Each $x\pm w$ is the average of two of the four selected child slopes. One of these four actual slopes is therefore greater than $r(1+g/2)$. Choose it and repeat through $k$ generations. This argument makes no claim that a supremum over all children is attained. ◻

</details>

A bound initially stated only for $\|y\|=t$ extends to $\|y\|\ge t$: for a fixed direction $v$, the function $h(r)=\max\{\|u+rv\|,\|u-rv\|\}$ is even and convex, and hence nondecreasing on $[0,\infty)$. This observation applies likewise to the average of the two norms.

<a id="stmt-met-full-gain"></a>
<div id="met:full-gain" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (The full asymptotic gain in a backward step). *Assume the finite-codimensional hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#met:quotient-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:quotient-transfer">18</a>. Consider a noncontracting embedding with upper factor $D\ge1$. If $t\le1/D$ and all child slopes of one parent have norm at most $L$, then $\|x\|\le L/(1+g)$. Consequently every depth-$k$ embedding with upper factor $D$ satisfies $1\le D/(1+g)^k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-full-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-full-gain</summary>

*Proof.* Put $r=\|x\|\in[1,D]$. Quotient compactness supplies half-differences $w$ with $\|w\|\ge1$ and vectors $v\in F_{x/r}$ with $\|v-w\|<\eta$, where $0<\eta<1/2$ is arbitrarily small. Set $w'=\|w\|v/\|v\|$. Then $w'\in F_{x/r}$, $\|w'\|/r=\|w\|/r\ge1/D\ge t$, and $$\|w'-w\|\le \bigl|\|w\|-\|v\|\bigr|+\|v-w\|<2\eta.$$ The intrinsic gain and the crossed-average child bound imply $(1+g)r\le\max_\pm\|x\pm w'\|\le L+2\eta$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$ before taking suprema over parents, then iterate backward. Without the norm-preserving rescaling, the same proof works under the smaller threshold $t\le1/(2D)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-local-amuc"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:local-amuc" class="lemma">

<a id="met:bridge:local-amuc-input"></a>
**Lemma 20** (The local midpoint estimate gives an enlarged lens gap). *Let $R>0$ and put $t=R/4$. Suppose $\gamma>0$ is such that every unit $u$ admits a closed finite-codimensional subspace $F_u$ with $$\label{met:bridge:local-amuc-input}
 \max_\pm\|u\pm t y\|\ge1+\gamma
 \qquad(y\in F_u,\ \|y\|=1).$$ Then $\beta=\gamma/2$ excludes infinite $R$-separated families in $M(u,\beta)$, uniformly over unit $u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-local-amuc"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-local-amuc</summary>

*Proof.* If $(z_i)\subset M(u,\beta)$ were such a family, symmetry and convexity would place every half-difference $(z_i-z_j)/2$ in the same lens. The family is bounded, so its image in the finite-dimensional quotient $X/F_u$ has arbitrarily close distinct pairs. Hence there are $i\ne j$ and $w\in F_u$ such that, with $v=(z_i-z_j)/2$, $$\|v\|\ge R/2,\qquad
 \|v-w\|<\min\{R/4,\gamma/2\}.$$ In particular $\|w\|>R/4=t$. For a fixed unit direction $y$, the function $s\mapsto\max_\pm\|u\pm s y\|$ is even and convex, hence nondecreasing for $s\ge0$. Apply <a href="#met:bridge:local-amuc-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:local-amuc-input">[met:bridge:local-amuc-input]</a> to $y=w/\|w\|$ to obtain $\max_\pm\|u\pm w\|\ge1+\gamma$. On the other hand, $v\in M(u,\beta)$ and the strict approximation error give $$\max_\pm\|u\pm w\|
 \le1+\beta+\|w-v\|<1+\gamma,$$ a contradiction. For $R=2/D$, Proposition <a href="#met:bridge:enlarged-backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:bridge:enlarged-backward">15</a> now gives <a href="#met:bridge:enlarged-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:enlarged-depth">[met:bridge:enlarged-depth]</a> with $\delta=\beta=\gamma/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="met:bridge:renorming-section"></a>
#### The obstruction for an equivalent midpoint-convex norm

The following obstruction is the countably branching diamond case of (F. Baudier et al. 2017, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. We include the short deduction from the quotient transfer to specify the norm convention and the effect of an equivalent norm.

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-equivalent-amuc"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:equivalent-amuc" class="theorem">

<a id="met:bridge:max-modulus"></a>
**Theorem 21**. *If a Banach space admits an equivalent asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex norm, then the family $(\mathcal D_k)_{k\ge0}$ does not embed into it with uniformly bounded distortion. Write $\operatorname{cof}(X)$ for the closed finite-codimensional linear subspaces of $X$. In infinite dimension, midpoint convexity may be expressed by positivity of the maximum modulus $$\label{met:bridge:max-modulus}
 \delta_N^{\max}(t)=
 \inf_{N(u)=1}\ 
 \sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(X)}\ 
 \inf_{\substack{y\in F\\N(y)=1}}
 \bigl(\max_\pm N(u\pm t y)-1\bigr)>0
 \qquad(0<t<1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-equivalent-amuc"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-equivalent-amuc</summary>

*Proof.* In finite dimension, even $\mathcal D_1$ has a bounded infinite separated family of midpoints, whose images under a bi-Lipschitz map would contradict total boundedness. Consider an infinite-dimensional space, and suppose $a\|x\|\le N(x)\le b\|x\|$ with $a,b>0$. Embeddings with common distortion $D_0$ for the original norm, and arbitrary positive scales, would give embeddings with common distortion $D=(b/a)D_0$ for $N$: divide each map by its original lower scale times $a$.

Fix $t=1/(2D)$ and $0<g<\delta_N^{\max}(t)$. The definition of the supremum in <a href="#met:bridge:max-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:max-modulus">[met:bridge:max-modulus]</a> supplies, for every $N$-unit $u$, a finite-codimensional $F_u$ on whose unit sphere the maximum is at least $1+g$. Even convexity in each direction extends this bound to every vector of $F_u$ with $N$-norm at least $t$. Proposition <a href="#met:quotient-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:quotient-transfer">18</a>, applied to $N$, therefore gives $D\ge(1+g/2)^k$ for every $k$, which is impossible. An averaged midpoint lower bound also supplies the required maximum bound, since the maximum dominates the average. ◻

</details>

More generally, if $a\|v\|\le N(v)\le b\|v\|$, an embedding with distortion $D$ for $N$ has distortion at most $(b/a)D$ for the original norm, and conversely with the same factor. Thus the absence of uniform diamond distortion is invariant under equivalent renorming, while the numerical constants attached to a given norm need not be.

<a id="met:d-section"></a>
### Graph consequences of path and segment costs

The next four models change what a tree representation costs: a sum of Hilbert and path costs, their Euclidean combination, a charge at each segment’s start, or a restriction to incomparable segments. These are different norms. Within one norm, several estimates can also give different bounds. We first extract their squared losses, then retain the actual-child or qualitative conclusions when the method supplies different information. All intrinsic inputs come from the companion on bounded tree potentials and path costs; the graph deductions use the transfers proved above.

<a id="met:linear-section"></a>
#### Linear path cost

Recall the linear path-cost space $X_{\rm lin}$ from Section <a href="#dir:layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:layers">6</a>. Its component norm is the infimum of $\|h\|_2+\|\mu\|_1$ over decompositions into a Hilbert vector and root paths, including the component root. For later comparison write $J_n\mu=\sum_s\mu(s)b_s$ for the root-path sum on $T_n=\mathbb N^{\le n}$. The finite ancestor-closed coordinate projections are contractive, finite supports are dense, and this space and its dual are separable and reflexive. The squared estimates below and the weakly null estimate in Section <a href="#met:weak-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:weak-linear">8.2</a> give lower bounds for this same norm; the latter will give the strongest coefficient displayed here.

The positive-flow argument gives, for $Pp=p$, $Py=0$ (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.7), <a id="met:linear-flow-input"></a>$$\label{met:linear-flow-input}
 \|y\|^2\le C^2(\|p+y\|^2+\|p-y\|^2-2\|p\|^2),
 \qquad C=6(\sqrt2+1).$$ A separate trimming argument (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.10) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] gives the same inequality with $C^2$ replaced by $242$. Since each endpoint is at most $R$, these are <a href="#met:square-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:square-input">[met:square-input]</a> with $a=1/(2C^2)$ and $a=1/484$, respectively. Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a> and Theorem <a href="#met:squared-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:squared-transfer">11</a> therefore give <a id="met:linear-flow-graph"></a>$$\label{met:linear-flow-graph}
 D^2\ge1+\frac{k}{2C^2},\qquad
 D^2\ge1+\frac{k}{484},$$ using the corresponding proof in each case. In upper-one normalization, the first argument says $r_j^2\le r_{j+1}^2-1/(2C^2D^2)$ and hence also retains $k\le2C^2D^2$.

The inward-clipping argument (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] gives the different parameterized estimate <a id="met:linear-clipping-input"></a>$$\label{met:linear-clipping-input}
 \max_\pm\|p\pm y\|
 \ge\frac{\|p\|+c\|y\|/2}{\sqrt{1+4c^2}}
 \qquad(c>0, Pp=p, Py=0).$$ Here is its direct actual-child application, retaining the error budget. For a parent $x$ in the noncontracting normalization set $$c=\frac1{8D},\quad Q=\sqrt{1+4c^2},\quad
 \gamma=\frac c2-(Q-1)D,\quad
 \eta=\frac{\gamma}{2(1+c/2+2Q)}.$$ Since $Q-1\le2c^2$, we have $\gamma\ge c/4>0$. Choose a finite head $P$ with $\|x-Px\|<\eta$, and choose the pair in Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>; write $w=(z_i-z_j)/2$ and $y=w-Pw$. The greatest of the four actual child norms is at least $$\begin{aligned}
 \max_\pm\|x\pm w\|
 &\ge \frac{\|Px\|+c\|y\|/2}{Q}-2\eta\\
 &\ge \frac{\|x\|-\eta+c(1-\eta)/2}{Q}-2\eta
 \ge\|x\|+\frac{\gamma}{2Q}.
\end{aligned}$$ Selecting such a child through all $k$ generations gives <a id="met:linear-clipping-graph"></a>$$\label{met:linear-clipping-graph}
 1+\frac{k\gamma}{2Q}\le D.$$ The additive increment is attached to the clipping estimate, independently of the positive-flow and trimming proofs.

<a id="met:weak-linear"></a>
#### Weakly null pairs for the linear norm

<a id="met:weak-input"></a>
<a id="met:linear-weak-graph"></a>
A further form of inward clipping (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.5) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] states that for every $x\in X_{\rm lin}$ and every bounded weakly null sequence $(y_j)$, $$\label{met:weak-input}
 \liminf_j\max_\pm\|x\pm y_j\|
 \ge\sqrt{\|x\|^2+\frac1{16}(\liminf_j\|y_j\|)^2}.$$ This input retains a larger squared loss when we pair deviations before applying it: $$\label{met:linear-weak-graph}
 D^2\ge1+k/16.$$

For a parent with child bound $R$ in the noncontracting normalization, the bounded sequence of deviations $z_i$ has a weakly Cauchy subsequence. Indeed, diagonalize its evaluations on a countable norm-dense subset of $X_{\rm lin}^*$, then use boundedness to approximate every other functional. Relabel this subsequence and set $w_j=(z_{2j}-z_{2j-1})/2$. It is weakly null, and sibling separation gives $\|w_j\|\ge1$. Crossed convex averages give $$x+w_j=\tfrac12((x+z_{2j})+(x-z_{2j-1})),\qquad
 x-w_j=\tfrac12((x-z_{2j})+(x+z_{2j-1})),$$ so both norms are at most $R$. Equation <a href="#met:weak-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:weak-input">[met:weak-input]</a> yields $\|x\|^2+1/16\le R^2$. Taking the stage suprema and summing proves <a href="#met:linear-weak-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:linear-weak-graph">[met:linear-weak-graph]</a>. Alternatively, reflexivity supplies a weak limit $z$ and one can use $(z_i-z)/2$. Separation then gives only $\liminf\|(z_i-z)/2\|\ge1/2$, yielding the weaker $D^2\ge1+k/64$; pairing two deviations avoids that loss.

The qualitative bottom-up mass-removal argument (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 5.9) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] can also be transferred without replacing it by <a href="#met:weak-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:weak-input">[met:weak-input]</a>. It states that pure-tail perturbations tend uniformly to zero as finite-head centers approach the unit sphere, allowing the heads to vary. Thus it satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#met:bridge:sequential-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:bridge:sequential-lens">17</a>, which supplies a uniform separated-lens gap. Proposition <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a> gives $D^{-1}\le\rho_D^k$ for some $\rho_D<1$. This route uses the qualitative input alone and requires no weak-limit extraction or numerical modulus.

<a id="met:quadratic-section"></a>
#### Quadratic path cost

<a id="met:quadratic-input"></a>
On the same trees define $$Q_n(z)=\inf_{z=h+J_n\mu}(\|h\|_2^2+\|\mu\|_1^2)^{1/2},\qquad
 X_{\rm quad}=\left(\bigoplus_{n\ge1}(\ell_2(T_n),Q_n)\right)_2.$$ This changes the norm: the root singleton has quadratic-cost norm $1/\sqrt2$, whereas its linear-cost norm is one. The two cancellation estimates (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 7.6 and Proposition 7.10) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] have the same arbitrary-displacement form, with constants $8$ and $7$: $$\label{met:quadratic-input}
 \|(I-P)y\|\le K\sqrt{R^2-\|p\|^2},
 \qquad Pp=p,\quad \|p\pm y\|\le R,\qquad K\in\{8,7\}.$$ Finite ancestral heads have bounded finite-rank projections, and finite coordinate vectors are dense. These facts permit the same packing argument for either constant. For an infinite $\varepsilon$-separated $R$-lens at arbitrary $x$, choose a finite-support $p$ with $\|p-x\|<\eta$. Apply <a href="#met:quadratic-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:quadratic-input">[met:quadratic-input]</a> to every deviation at radius $R+\eta$, using the same finite-rank head $P$. Two head projections are arbitrarily close. Hence $$\varepsilon\le2K\sqrt{(R+\eta)^2-\|p\|^2}.$$ Letting $\eta$ decrease to zero gives $\|x\|^2\le R^2-\varepsilon^2/(4K^2)$. Thus Theorem <a href="#met:squared-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:squared-transfer">11</a> gives $D^2\ge1+k/K^2$. In particular, <a id="met:quadratic-graph"></a>$$\label{met:quadratic-graph}
 D^2\ge1+k/49.$$ The coefficient-$8$ proof independently gives $D^2\ge1+k/64$. Only boundedness and finite rank of $P$ entered the packing step.

The separate stopped-flow energy estimate (OpenAI 2026a, Proposition 7.8) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] has input $$\|p\|^2+\|y\|^2/144
 \le\tfrac12(\|p+y\|^2+\|p-y\|^2),
 \qquad Pp=p,\quad Py=0.$$ It gives $r_j^2\le r_{j+1}^2-1/(144D^2)$ in upper-one normalization, and hence the quantitative necessary condition $k\le144D^2$. The finite-head pairing, the $R+2\eta$ endpoint error, and the limiting step are exactly those in Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a>.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Packing at a fixed radius.

The same arbitrary-tail input also allows a direct finite-error packing argument. Its coefficient-$7$ version gives, for $\alpha=1/D$ and $\delta=\alpha^2/2000$, the backward factor $1-\delta$. Indeed, if a unit-ball lens had center norm greater than $1-\delta$, approximate it within $\delta$ by $p$. Then $\|p\|>1-2\delta$ and the endpoint radius is $1+\delta$. Its common tails have norm at most $$7\sqrt{(1+\delta)^2-(1-2\delta)^2}
 \le7\sqrt{6\delta}<\alpha/2.$$ Two head projections within $\alpha$ contradict separation $2\alpha$. For the pruning estimate with coefficient $9$ (OpenAI 2026a, Proposition 7.12) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)], choose instead <a id="met:pruning-parameter"></a>$$\label{met:pruning-parameter}
 0<\gamma<1/2,\qquad
 9\sqrt{(1+\gamma)^2-(1-2\gamma)^2}<\frac{2\alpha}{3}.$$ The same approximation bounds every tail by $2\alpha/3$; choose two head projections within $2\alpha/3$. Their full distance is less than $2\alpha$, a contradiction. Thus the backward factor is $1-\gamma$. These choices retain the bounds supplied by their respective proofs.

The threshold-removal argument (OpenAI 2026a, Proposition 7.14) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] gives a different relative-radius input: for every nonzero $x$ and $0<\delta\le1$, a single finite head bounds all deviations satisfying $\|x\pm y\|\le(1+\delta)\|x\|$ by $$\|(I-P)y\|\le72\sqrt\delta\,\|x\|.$$ Take $\delta=(200D)^{-2}$. If every child of a noncontracting parent had norm at most $(1+\delta)\|x\|$, their deviations would have a common tail bound $72\sqrt\delta D$. Two head projections can be chosen within one, giving full distance less than $1+144\sqrt\delta D<2$. This contradicts sibling separation. An actual child therefore grows by the factor $1+\delta$, and $D\ge(1+\delta)^k$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### A finite-codimensional input.

The component-to-sum argument (OpenAI 2026a, Lemma 7.15, Proposition 7.16 and Corollary 7.17) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] for the quadratic norm gives, at arbitrary unit centers, a finite-codimensional subspace on which $$\max_\pm\|x\pm y\|\le1+e
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|y\|\le17(2e)^{1/6},
 \qquad0<e\le1/16.$$ Given $D$, choose $e$ so that $17(2e)^{1/6}<1/(2D)$. Proposition <a href="#met:quotient-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:quotient-transfer">18</a> then gives an actual child gain $1+e/2$, and $D\ge(1+e/2)^k$. This application uses the proved component-to-sum estimate, including its zero-center cases, rather than an assertion that passing to an outer Hilbert sum automatically preserves a midpoint gap.

<a id="met:segment-section"></a>
#### Segments with prescribed start cost

For nonnegative start masses $m_s$ on $T_n$, let $q_s=m_s+(\sum_{c\text{ child of }s}q_c^2)^{1/2}$, computed upward from the leaves. A finite or countable signed segment representation $z=\sum_j a_j\mathbf1_{[s_j,t_j]}$, with absolutely convergent output at every coordinate, has start masses $m_s=\sum_{j:s_j=s}|a_j|$ and cost $q_o$ at the root. Take the infimum over all such representations of finite cost and complete; the outer Hilbert sum of the resulting finite-height spaces is denoted $X_{\rm start}$. Its intrinsic inequality is (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 8.4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] <a id="met:segment-input"></a>$$\label{met:segment-input}
 \max_\pm\|p\pm y\|^2\ge\|p\|^2+\|y\|^2/64,
 \qquad P_Cp=p,\quad P_Cy=0,$$ where $C$ is a finite ancestral head. Coordinate projections onto such heads are bounded and finite supports are dense.

For a noncontracting parent $x$ let $M$ be the supremum of its child norms. For arbitrarily small $\eta>0$, choose $p$ and the paired tail $y$ by Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a>, with $\|y\|\ge1-\eta$. Then $$M\ge\sqrt{\|p\|^2+(1-\eta)^2/64}-2\eta.$$ On letting $\eta$ decrease to zero, $M^2\ge\|x\|^2+1/64$. Apply this to every parent before taking the stage suprema. Summing their squared losses gives <a id="met:segment-graph"></a>$$\label{met:segment-graph}
 D^2\ge1+k/64.$$ If one instead wants to follow actual children, some child has squared norm greater than $\|x\|^2+1/128$: otherwise the supremum would be at most this strictly smaller number. Selecting an actual child at each generation retains the separate bound $D^2\ge1+k/128$. No contractivity of $I-P_C$ was used, and no child was presumed to attain the supremum.

<a id="met:incomparable-section"></a>
#### Incomparable segments

Let $Y_I$ be the completion on the disjoint union of $T_n$ for the supremum of Euclidean sums of segment evaluations, where the admissible segments are pairwise incomparable: every node of one segment is incomparable with every node of another. Put $X_I=Y_I^*$. This restriction differs from requiring the segments merely to be disjoint. The intrinsic estimate for $X_I$ (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 9.5) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[TreePotentials](#ref-TreePotentials)] is $$\|(I-P_E)y\|\le K\sqrt{1-\|p\|},\qquad
 P_Ep=p,\quad \|p\pm y\|\le1,\quad K=\sqrt2+\sqrt6,$$ where $E$ is finite and ancestral and $y$ may have head coordinates. Finite ancestral projections are contractive and finite coordinate vectors are dense. For a pure tail, homogeneity gives $\|y\|^2\le K^2R(R-\|p\|)$ at radius $R$. Proposition <a href="#met:tail-to-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:tail-to-lens">10</a> and Theorem <a href="#met:squared-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:squared-transfer">11</a> therefore give <a id="met:incomparable-additive"></a>$$\label{met:incomparable-additive}
 k\le K^2D(D-1).$$ At radius one the same lens estimate reads, for an infinite $2\alpha$-separated family, $$\|x\|\le1-(\alpha/K)^2.$$ Under lower factor $\alpha=1/D$ and upper factor one, normalize each parent lens by its child bound $R\le1$. Its separation is at least $2\alpha/R\ge2\alpha$, so every parent bound contracts by $\rho=1-(\alpha/K)^2$. Hence $\alpha\le\rho^k$ by Proposition <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a>. This is an application to the incomparable-segment norm itself, regardless of the equality of its displayed tail constant with one obtained for the disjoint-segment norm.

<a id="met:f:applications"></a>
### Graph consequences of recursive upper-array norms

Recursive upper arrays give three further target norms. The first uses a height-dependent child exponent and an outer Hilbert sum; its sixth- and cubic-power estimates yield additive losses in squared slope norm. The other two use Euclidean child aggregation on a joined tree and differ at the joining root. We retain the qualitative propagation argument for the free-root norm and an explicit finite-error cubic argument for the fixed-zero-root norm. The constants below belong to these stated inputs and deductions.

Let $T_h=\bigcup_{j=0}^h\mathbb N^j$ and $p_h=1+1/h$. For real $v\in\ell_{p_h}(T_h)$, let $P(v),Q(v)$ be the least nonnegative arrays whose difference is $v$ and which satisfy $$P_s(v)\ge\Big(\sum_{t\in C(s)}P_t(v)^{p_h}\Big)^{1/p_h},
 \qquad
 Q_s(v)\ge\Big(\sum_{t\in C(s)}Q_t(v)^{p_h}\Big)^{1/p_h}.$$ Here $C(s)$ is the set of children of $s$ and the sums at leaves are zero. More explicitly, if the two child norms are $a_s,b_s$, then $P_s(v)=\max\{a_s,v_s+b_s\}$ and $Q_s(v)=\max\{a_s-v_s,b_s\}$. Write $$\nu_h(v)=P_\emptyset(v)+Q_\emptyset(v),\qquad
 X_{\mathrm v}=\Big(\bigoplus_{h\ge1}(\ell_{p_h}(T_h),\nu_h)
                  \Big)_{\ell_2}.$$ The intrinsic upper-array results supply completeness, reflexivity, separability of $X_{\mathrm v}^*$, density of finite coordinate vectors, and the estimates used below; see (OpenAI 2026b, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)]. We use the slopes from <a href="#met:slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:slopes">[met:slopes]</a>. To discuss paired children, write <a id="met:f:slopes"></a>$$\label{met:f:slopes}
 y_j=x+z_j,\qquad \bar y_j=x-z_j.$$ In noncontracting normalization the $y_j$, and also the $z_j$, are $2$-separated. In upper-one normalization the separation is $2/D$.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The sixth-power estimate

A finite head in $X_{\mathrm v}$ means a finite ancestor-closed set in each of finitely many components, containing the component root whenever that component is used. Its coordinate projection is a bounded finite-rank operator. No estimate on its operator norm is needed in the following argument.

The finite-head estimate in (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 7.4) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)] allows arbitrary completed tails.

<a id="stmt-met-f-sixth"></a>
<div id="met:f:sixth" class="proposition">

<a id="met:f:sixth-input"></a>
<a id="met:f:sixth-power-depth"></a>
<a id="met:f:sixth-depth"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *For $X_{\mathrm v}$ one has $$\label{met:f:sixth-input}
 S-2\|x\|_{\mathrm v}^2
 \ge c_6S^{-2}\|y\|_{\mathrm v}^6,
 \qquad
 S=\|x+y\|_{\mathrm v}^2+\|x-y\|_{\mathrm v}^2,
 \qquad c_6=2\exp(-(\mathrm e-1))4^{-6},$$ whenever $x$ is supported on a finite head and $y$ vanishes there. The right side is interpreted as zero when $S=0$. Every distortion-$D$ embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ into this norm satisfies $$\label{met:f:sixth-power-depth}
 \frac{c_6 k}{8}\le D^6-D^4.$$ For $0<\varepsilon\le1$, put <a id="met:f:sixth-q"></a>$$\label{met:f:sixth-q}
 q_6(\varepsilon)=
 \left(1-\frac{c_6\varepsilon^6}{512}\right)^{1/2}.$$ Every infinite family satisfying $$\|x+u_j\|_{\mathrm v},\ \|x-u_j\|_{\mathrm v}\le1,
 \qquad \|u_j-u_l\|_{\mathrm v}\ge\varepsilon\quad(j\ne l)$$ has $\|x\|_{\mathrm v}\le q_6(\varepsilon)$. Keeping a fixed unit-lens contraction gives the additional form $$\label{met:f:sixth-depth}
 D^{-1}\le q_6(D^{-1})^k.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-f-sixth"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-f-sixth</summary>

*Proof.* If $\|x\pm y\|_{\mathrm v}\le R$ with $R>0$, then $S\le2R^2$. For $S>0$, <a href="#met:f:sixth-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sixth-input">[met:f:sixth-input]</a> implies $$R^2\ge\|x\|_{\mathrm v}^2+
             \frac{c_6}{8R^4}\|y\|_{\mathrm v}^6.$$ It is trivial when $S=0$. Theorem <a href="#met:power-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:power-transfer">12</a>, with power $6$ and coefficient $c_6/8$, gives <a href="#met:f:sixth-power-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sixth-power-depth">[met:f:sixth-power-depth]</a>.

For the unit-lens statement, Lemma <a href="#met:finite-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:finite-pair">2</a> supplies pure head–tail pairs $x',y'$ with $\|x'-x\|_{\mathrm v}<\tau$, $\|y'\|_{\mathrm v}\ge\varepsilon/2-\tau$, and endpoints of norm at most $1+2\tau$. Apply the displayed homogeneous estimate and let $\tau\downarrow0$. It gives $\|x\|_{\mathrm v}^2+c_6\varepsilon^6/512\le1$. In upper-one graph normalization use the fixed separation parameter $\varepsilon=1/D$; actual sibling separation is at least $2/D$. Proposition <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a> proves <a href="#met:f:sixth-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sixth-depth">[met:f:sixth-depth]</a>. ◻

</details>

The power bound keeps the child radius in the loss at every level. The geometric form instead uses one contraction factor chosen at the start from $D$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### The cubic estimate and weakly null paired differences

The intrinsic cubic inequality and its weakly null consequence are (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 7.6 and Proposition 7.7) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)]. The first gives another additive power bound. The second exhibits a different selection: pairing a weakly Cauchy subsequence. The paired differences and crossed convex averages also occur in the approximate midpoint argument of (Dilworth et al. 2016, proof of Corollary 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]; here dual separability supplies the subsequence by diagonal extraction.

<a id="stmt-met-f-cubic-weak"></a>
<div id="met:f:cubic-weak" class="proposition">

<a id="met:f:cubic-input"></a>
<a id="met:f:cubic-power-depth"></a>
<a id="met:f:cubic-depth"></a>
**Proposition 23**. *The dual $X_{\mathrm v}^*$ is separable, and under the same finite-head support conditions as above one has $$\label{met:f:cubic-input}
 \|x\pm y\|_{\mathrm v}\le R
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 R^2\ge\|x\|_{\mathrm v}^2+
       \frac{c_3}{2\sqrt2\,R}\|y\|_{\mathrm v}^3,
 \qquad R>0,\quad c_3=(6\mathrm e)^{-3}.$$ Consequently every distortion-$D$ embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ satisfies $$\label{met:f:cubic-power-depth}
 \frac{c_3 k}{2\sqrt2}\le D^3-D.$$ For $0<\varepsilon\le1$, define <a id="met:f:cubic-theta"></a>$$\label{met:f:cubic-theta}
 \theta(\varepsilon)=
 \left(1-\frac{c_3\varepsilon^3}{2\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}.$$ If $(h_j)$ is weakly null, $\|h_j\|_{\mathrm v}\ge\varepsilon$, and $\|x\pm h_j\|_{\mathrm v}\le1$, then $\|x\|_{\mathrm v}\le\theta(\varepsilon)$. Every distortion-$D$ embedding of the depth-$k$ countably branching diamond into $X_{\mathrm v}$ satisfies $$\label{met:f:cubic-depth}
 1\le D\theta(D^{-1})^k.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-f-cubic-weak"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-f-cubic-weak</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#met:power-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:power-transfer">12</a>, applied directly to <a href="#met:f:cubic-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:cubic-input">[met:f:cubic-input]</a>, gives <a href="#met:f:cubic-power-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:cubic-power-depth">[met:f:cubic-power-depth]</a>. The weakly null implication is the cited Proposition 7.7. To use it in the graph, normalize to lower factor one and upper factor $D$, and let $R\le D$ bound the children of one parent $x$. Dual separability gives a weakly Cauchy subsequence of $(y_j)$. The diagonal argument is the one in Section <a href="#met:weak-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:weak-linear">8.2</a>.

Take successive disjoint pairs $y_{j_{2r}},y_{j_{2r+1}}$ from this subsequence and put $$h_r=\frac{y_{j_{2r}}-y_{j_{2r+1}}}{2R}.$$ The sequence $(h_r)$ is weakly null. Sibling separation gives $\|h_r\|_{\mathrm v}\ge1/R\ge1/D$. The identities $$\frac{x}{R}+h_r
   =\frac{y_{j_{2r}}+\bar y_{j_{2r+1}}}{2R},
 \qquad
 \frac{x}{R}-h_r
   =\frac{y_{j_{2r+1}}+\bar y_{j_{2r}}}{2R}$$ show that both points belong to the unit ball. The weakly null conclusion, with $\varepsilon=1/D$, gives $\|x\|_{\mathrm v}
\le R\theta(D^{-1})$. Taking the parent suprema and iterating this contraction proves <a href="#met:f:cubic-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:cubic-depth">[met:f:cubic-depth]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### A qualitative contraction for the free-root sum norm

We next change the norm. Join disjoint copies of $T_h$ at a new root $\rho$ whose children are their roots. At every node use the least nonnegative arrays $p,q$ with difference equal to the coordinate and with each array dominating the $\ell_2$ norm of its child values. For a finitely supported vector with an unrestricted root coordinate, put $$N_J(x)=p_\rho(x)+q_\rho(x),$$ and let $X_J$ be the completion. In this subsection $X_J$ refers to the tree formed by joining these finite-height components, with a free root coordinate. In particular, the norm of an indicator of an initial path starting at a component root is two.

Restriction $P_H$ to a finite ancestral set containing $\rho$ is contractive. On finitely supported arrays, restriction removes child values outside $H$; the recursion is monotone in their nonnegative norms, so neither root field can increase. This proves contractivity on the dense finite arrays and hence on the completion. Applying $I-P_H$ to finite approximations also proves density of finitely supported vectors in $\ker P_H$.

<a id="met:f:sum-input"></a>
The intrinsic probabilistic propagation estimate (OpenAI 2026b, Lemmas 6.1 and 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)] supplies, for every $0<\zeta\le2$, a number $\gamma(2,\zeta/4)>0$ such that $$\label{met:f:sum-input}
 \frac{N_J(x+y)+N_J(x-y)}2
 \ge N_J(x)+2\gamma(2,\zeta/4)$$ whenever $x,y$ are finitely supported, $x$ is supported on a finite ancestor-closed set $H$ containing $\rho$, $y$ vanishes on $H$, $N_J(x\pm y)\le2$, and $N_J(y)\ge\zeta$. The number $\gamma$ is uniform in the finite tree and its height. The estimate comes from the logarithmic-martingale propagation of the two upper arrays; only its positivity is used here.

The resulting separated-lens statement is also (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 6.3) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)].

<a id="stmt-met-f-sum"></a>
<div id="met:f:sum" class="proposition">

<a id="met:f:sum-depth"></a>
**Proposition 24**. *For every $0<\varepsilon\le2$ there is $\eta(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)$ such that $$N_J(x\pm z_j)\le1,
 \qquad N_J(z_j-z_l)\ge\varepsilon\quad(j\ne l)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 N_J(x)\le1-\eta(\varepsilon).$$ For $\varepsilon>2$ the hypotheses are impossible, so any $\eta(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)$ is admissible. An embedding of the depth-$k$ countably branching diamond into $X_J$ with distortion at most $D$ therefore satisfies $$\label{met:f:sum-depth}
 D^{-1}\le\bigl(1-\eta(2/D)\bigr)^k.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-f-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-f-sum</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $\varepsilon$ and put $\zeta=\varepsilon/4$. For each sufficiently small $\delta>0$, take a finite coordinate vector $x'$ with $N_J(x-x')<\delta$ and a finite ancestor-closed head $H$ containing its support and $\rho$. The sequence $(z_j)$ is bounded. Finite-dimensional total boundedness therefore supplies a pair with $$u=(z_j-z_l)/2,
 \qquad N_J(u)\ge\varepsilon/2,
 \qquad N_J(P_Hu)<\delta.$$ Also $N_J(x\pm u)\le1$ by convexity. Approximate $(I-P_H)u$ by a finitely supported vector $y$ vanishing on $H$ so that $N_J(y-u)<2\delta$. This is possible by first using density and then applying the bounded operator $I-P_H$; no contractivity is assumed. For small enough $\delta$ we have $$N_J(y)\ge\zeta,
 \qquad N_J(x'\pm y)\le1+3\delta\le2.$$ Now <a href="#met:f:sum-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sum-input">[met:f:sum-input]</a> gives $$1+3\delta\ge N_J(x')+2\gamma(2,\varepsilon/16)
 \ge N_J(x)-\delta+2\gamma(2,\varepsilon/16).$$ Let $\delta\downarrow0$. For example, $\eta(\varepsilon)=\min\{\gamma(2,\varepsilon/16),1/2\}$ has the required property. This choice records positivity, not a numerical power law in $\varepsilon$.

In upper-one normalization use the fixed separation $2/D$ and apply Proposition <a href="#met:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:backward">14</a> with $\rho=1-\eta(2/D)$. This proves <a href="#met:f:sum-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sum-depth">[met:f:sum-depth]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### An additive cubic loss for the fixed-zero-root norm

On the same joined tree, now require $x_\rho=0$ and define $$N_0(x)=p_\rho(x)=q_\rho(x)
 =\max\left\{
       \Big(\sum_h p_{r_h}(x)^2\Big)^{1/2},
       \Big(\sum_h q_{r_h}(x)^2\Big)^{1/2}\right\},$$ where $r_h$ is the root of the $h$th component. Denote its completion by $X_0$. The root coordinate is fixed at zero and the cost is the common root value. On the root-zero subspace the preceding sum norm is $2N_0$; keeping the actual normalization is essential for the constants below. The same ancestral projections preserve this subspace and are contractive for $N_0$; finitely supported tails are dense here as well.

<a id="met:f:zero-input"></a>
The deterministic upper-array estimate for $X_0$ is (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 2.1, equation (2.4)) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[RecursivePotentials](#ref-RecursivePotentials)]: $$\label{met:f:zero-input}
 \frac{N_0(x+y)+N_0(x-y)}2
 \ge N_0(x)+\frac{N_0(y)^3}{8(2N_0(x)+N_0(y))^2},$$ for every finite ancestor-closed head containing $\rho$, every $x$ supported on it, and every nonzero completed tail $y$ vanishing there. Finite support is required of $x$, but not of $y$.

<a id="stmt-met-f-zero"></a>
<div id="met:f:zero" class="proposition">

<a id="met:f:zero-depth"></a>
**Proposition 25**. *If the depth-$k$ countably branching diamond admits an embedding into $X_0$ with distortion at most $D\ge1$, then $$\label{met:f:zero-depth}
 k\le4608D^3(1-D^{-1}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-f-zero"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-f-zero</summary>

*Proof.* Normalize to lower constant $A=1/D$ and upper constant one. For each level $i$, let $L_i$ be the supremum of the $N_0$-norms of all its historical edge slopes. Thus $A\le L_i\le1$. Fix any parent slope $x$ at level $i<k$. Its child slopes satisfy $N_0(y_j),N_0(\bar y_j)\le L_{i+1}$ and $N_0(y_j-y_l)\ge2A$ for $j\ne l$. For such a pair put $z=(y_j-y_l)/2$. Then <a id="met:f:zero-pair"></a>$$\label{met:f:zero-pair}
 A\le N_0(z)\le1,
 \qquad N_0(x\pm z)\le L_{i+1},$$ because $x+z=(y_j+\bar y_l)/2$ and $x-z=(y_l+\bar y_j)/2$.

Fix the errors before selecting the finite head and the pair: $$c_0=\frac{A^3}{2304},\qquad
 \eta=\frac{c_0}{6}.$$ Since $0<A\le1$, we have $\eta\le A/2$ and $\eta\le1$. Choose a finitely supported $x'$ with $N_0(x-x')\le\eta$, and then a finite ancestor-closed head $H$ containing its support and $\rho$. The projected child slopes $P_Hy_j$ form a bounded subset of a finite-dimensional space. Choose $j\ne l$ so that their half-difference $z$ satisfies $N_0(P_Hz)\le\eta$, and put $y=z-P_Hz$. Then $$A/2\le N_0(y)\le1+\eta,
 \qquad N_0(x')\le1+\eta.$$ In particular $y\ne0$, so <a href="#met:f:zero-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:zero-input">[met:f:zero-input]</a> applies to $x',y$. Its gain is at least $$\frac{(A/2)^3}{8\,[3(1+\eta)]^2}
 \ge\frac{(A/2)^3}{8\cdot6^2}
 =\frac{A^3}{2304}=c_0.$$ For both signs, $N_0(x\pm z)\ge N_0(x'\pm y)-2\eta$, and $N_0(x')\ge N_0(x)-\eta$. Consequently $$\begin{split}
 L_{i+1}
 &\ge\frac{N_0(x+z)+N_0(x-z)}2\\
 &\ge N_0(x)+c_0-3\eta
  =N_0(x)+\frac{c_0}{2}.
 \end{split}$$ The parent edge was arbitrary. Taking the supremum over all parent edges gives $L_i\le L_{i+1}-c_0/2$, without requiring that a supremum be attained. Iteration yields $$A\le L_0\le L_k-\frac{kc_0}{2}
 \le1-\frac{kA^3}{4608},$$ which is <a href="#met:f:zero-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:zero-depth">[met:f:zero-depth]</a>. ◻

</details>

The bounds for $X_{\mathrm v}$ distinguish an additive power budget from a contraction chosen at a fixed separation. For the Euclidean joined-tree models, <a href="#met:f:sum-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:sum-depth">[met:f:sum-depth]</a> records the positivity obtained by probabilistic propagation, while <a href="#met:f:zero-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:f:zero-depth">[met:f:zero-depth]</a> retains the explicit approximation errors in a deterministic cubic-gain argument. Each conclusion rules out a distortion bound independent of the diamond depth, in its stated norm.

<a id="met:forest-variants"></a>
### Alternative midpoint estimates for the segment forest

The joint quadratic estimate gives the strongest forest bound in this paper. The following inputs come from different intrinsic arguments and illustrate the transfer rules of Section <a href="#met:transfer-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:transfer-section">7</a>. They control radial deficit, parameterized test gains, or tails selected by sequential compactness and a common primal norming test.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Radial deficit

<a id="stmt-met-atomic-distortion"></a>
<div id="met:atomic-distortion" class="corollary">

**Corollary 26** (The atomic forest bound). *For the segment forest space $X$, every map satisfying <a href="#met:all-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:all-pairs">[met:all-pairs]</a> has $D^2\ge1+k/16$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-atomic-distortion"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-atomic-distortion</summary>

*Proof.* For the forest atomic estimate, the constant $C_*=\sqrt2+\sqrt6<4$ in the companion’s arbitrary-displacement radial estimate (OpenAI 2026c, Proposition 12.1) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)] gives <a href="#met:radial-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:radial-input">[met:radial-input]</a>. In particular, $$\varepsilon^2/64\le R(R-\|x\|)\le R^2-\|x\|^2,
 \qquad D^2\ge1+k/16.$$ This radial input gives a separate proof from the joint quadratic estimate. ◻

</details>

Keeping the exact constant in the same calculation also gives $k\le C_*^2D(D-1)$. Different atomic derivations with their own radial constant $C$ give <a href="#met:additive-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:additive-bound">[met:additive-bound]</a> with that constant; replacing one proof’s $C$ by another proof’s value would discard its quantitative content.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Parameterized clipping tests

Apply Proposition <a href="#met:parametric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:parametric">13</a> with $a=1/2$ and $b=3$, as in the clipping input of (OpenAI 2026c, sec. 10.1, Equation (10.9)) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)]. Take $\lambda=1/(6D)$; $Q-1\le3\lambda^2/2$ gives $\Delta\ge1/(24D)$. Thus the full parameterized consequence is <a id="met:parametric-twentyfour"></a>$$\label{met:parametric-twentyfour}
 1+\frac{k}{24D}\le D.$$ For an optimized input $\max_\pm\|p\pm y\|\ge\sqrt{\|p\|^2+a\|y\|^2}$, the same limiting selection gives a child supremum at least $\sqrt{\|x\|^2+a}$. Since $\sqrt{r^2+a}-r$ decreases with $r>0$, some actual child has norm at least $\|x\|+g/2$, where $g=\sqrt{D^2+a}-D>0$. With $a=1/12$, as in (OpenAI 2026c, Proposition 10.4 and Equation (10.10)) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)], this gives $1+kg/2\le D$. For this value of $a$, one may make the finite selection before passing to a limit by taking approximation error $\eta=g/8$: the norm comparison costs at most $4\eta$, leaving $g/2$. Both descriptions retain the same actual-child loss.

Likewise, a radial input with constant $C$ gives a child supremum at least $\|x\|+1/(C^2D)$. Selecting an actual child above the smaller threshold $\|x\|+1/(2C^2D)$ gives $1+k/(2C^2D)<D$ for $k\ge1$. The forest estimate in (OpenAI 2026c, Proposition 20.1) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)] permits $C=8$, giving the distinct supremal gain $1/(64D)$ and actual gain greater than $1/(128D)$.

<a id="met:bridge:fixed-bound-section"></a>
#### An additive gain for a fixed distortion bound

<a id="met:bridge:fixed-bound-input"></a>
The two-test estimate for an infinite bounded separated family in the segment forest has the following form (OpenAI 2026c, Proposition 11.3) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)]: for each $D\ge1$ there is $c_D>0$ such that $$\label{met:bridge:fixed-bound-input}
 \begin{gathered}
 1\le\|x\|\le D,\qquad \|z_i\|\le2D,\qquad
 \|z_i-z_j\|\ge1\quad(i\ne j)\\
 \Longrightarrow\quad
 \max_\pm\|x\pm z_i\|>\|x\|+c_D
 \quad\text{for some }i.
 \end{gathered}$$ For fixed $D$, the same $c_D$ works for every center and every family satisfying the displayed assumptions. The selected deviation need not annihilate the head.

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-fixed-bound"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:fixed-bound" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27**. *If a target satisfies <a href="#met:bridge:fixed-bound-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:fixed-bound-input">[met:bridge:fixed-bound-input]</a>, every noncontracting embedding of $\mathcal D_k$ with upper factor $D$ obeys $$1+kc_D\le D.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-fixed-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-fixed-bound</summary>

*Proof.* For every parent slope $x$ and its deviations $z_i$ from <a href="#met:slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:slopes">[met:slopes]</a>, one has $1\le\|x\|\le D$, $\|z_i\|\le D\le2D$, and $\|z_i-z_j\|\ge2\ge1$. The input therefore selects an actual child slope with norm greater than $\|x\|+c_D$. Starting at the original slope and making this choice through $k$ replacements gives a final slope greater than $1+kc_D$ when $k\ge1$, and every final slope is at most $D$. For $k=0$ the conclusion is $1\le D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Coordinate-null and sequential estimates

Use the upper-one normalization with $\alpha=1/D$. At a parent with child bound $0<R\le1$, its deviations satisfy $\|x\pm z_i\|\le R$ and $\|z_i-z_j\|\ge2\alpha$ for $i\ne j$. On the countable forest coordinate set they admit a subsequence whose coordinates all converge, by successive extraction and a diagonal choice. Pairing successive terms gives $w_j=(z_{2j}-z_{2j-1})/2$ with coordinates tending to zero, $\|w_j\|\ge\alpha$, and $\|x\pm w_j\|\le R$. If the intrinsic estimate gives $$\limsup_j\|w_j\|\le C\sqrt{R(R-\|x\|)},$$ then $$\|x\|\le R\left(1-\frac{\alpha^2}{C^2R^2}\right)
 \le R(1-\alpha^2/C^2),\qquad0<R\le1.$$ The forest coordinate-null estimate (OpenAI 2026c, Corollary 23.5) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)] has $C=\sqrt2+2\sqrt6<7$. Using $C=7$ retains the $R$-dependent intermediate inequality and gives the backward factor $1-1/(49D^2)$. Coordinatewise convergence here is used only with the stated intrinsic estimate; it is not silently replaced by weak convergence.

The qualitative sequential cancellation theorem for the forest (OpenAI 2026c, Lemma 23.2) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)] satisfies the varying-head hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#met:bridge:sequential-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="met:bridge:sequential-lens">17</a>. Hence it also gives, for each fixed $D$, a uniform unit-lens gap and a backward bound $D^{-1}\le\rho^k$. This application uses only sequential vanishing, without a quantitative power law.

<a id="met:bridge:normer-section"></a>
#### Choosing the norming test before the head and the pair

<a id="met:bridge:weighted-input"></a>
For the segment forest, the weighted endpoint estimate in the companion (OpenAI 2026c, Theorem 16.1 and equation (16.2)) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[SegmentLenses](#ref-SegmentLenses)] gives the following particular input. Let $f$ be a finitely supported primal vector with $\rho(f)\le1$, let $H$ be finite and ancestral with $\operatorname{supp}f\subset H$, and suppose $z_+,z_-\in B_X$. Put $$x=(z_++z_-)/2,\qquad
 d=1-x(f),\qquad a=\|Q_Hx\|.$$ Then $d\ge0$ and $$\label{met:bridge:weighted-input}
 \|Q_Hz_\pm\|
 \le2\sqrt{(a+\sqrt{2d})^2+4d}+2\sqrt d.$$ The same test $f$ is used for both endpoint laws; their individual deficits are nonnegative and at most $2d$.

<a id="stmt-met-bridge-normer-first"></a>
<div id="met:bridge:normer-first" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28**. *For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $0<\rho<1$ such that an infinite $\varepsilon$-separated symmetric unit lens in the segment forest space has center norm at most $\rho$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-met-bridge-normer-first"><summary>Proof of stmt-met-bridge-normer-first</summary>

*Proof.* Otherwise choose centers $x_n\in B_X$ with $\|x_n\|\to1$ and infinite $\varepsilon$-separated families $(v_{n,i})_i$ satisfying $\|x_n\pm v_{n,i}\|\le1$. First choose a finitely supported primal near-normer $f_n$ with $$\rho(f_n)\le1,\qquad x_n(f_n)>\|x_n\|-1/n.$$ Next choose a finite ancestral $H_n$ containing $\operatorname{supp}f_n$ and satisfying $\|Q_{H_n}x_n\|<1/n$. This is possible by density of finite coordinate vectors in $X$ and contractivity of $Q_{H_n}$: include the support of an approximation to $x_n$ before taking its finite ancestral closure.

<a id="met:bridge:normer-selection"></a>
Only now choose two members of the $n$th lens whose projections onto $H_n$ differ by less than $1/n$, and set $u_n=(v_{n,i}-v_{n,j})/2$. Finite-dimensional total boundedness justifies this choice, and crossed convex averages give $$\label{met:bridge:normer-selection}
 \|x_n\pm u_n\|\le1,\qquad
 \|P_{H_n}u_n\|<\frac1{2n},\qquad
 \|Q_{H_n}u_n\|\ge\frac\varepsilon2-\frac1{2n}.$$ Apply <a href="#met:bridge:weighted-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:weighted-input">[met:bridge:weighted-input]</a> with the already chosen $f_n,H_n$ and with $z_\pm=x_n\pm u_n$. Here $d_n=1-x_n(f_n)\to0$ and $a_n=\|Q_{H_n}x_n\|\to0$. Consequently both endpoint tails tend to zero. Their half-difference $Q_{H_n}u_n$ also tends to zero, contradicting <a href="#met:bridge:normer-selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="met:bridge:normer-selection">[met:bridge:normer-selection]</a>. No head has been chosen from the selected pair; the order is near-normer, head, and then pair. ◻

</details>

For the original upper-one graph normalization, retain the separation parameter $\varepsilon=1/D$. The normalized sibling deviations have separation at least $2/(DL)\ge1/D$ when the child bound is $0<L\le1$. The proposition gives the parent bound $\rho L$. Backward iteration therefore gives $1/D\le\rho^k$, with one fixed $\rho$ for this $D$.

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>

</div>

Girardi, Maria. 2001. “The Dual of the James Tree Space Is Asymptotically Uniformly Convex.” *Studia Mathematica* 147 (2): 119–30. <https://doi.org/10.4064/sm147-2-2>.

      "caption": "One normalized diamond replacement, with only three of the countably many paths shown. Each new edge has length $ℓ/2$. The old endpoints remain at distance $ℓ$, and any two distinct new middle vertices also have distance $ℓ$. The drawing is schematic.",
      "figure_id": "fig:diamond",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:diamond",
          "exact_quote": "One normalized diamond replacement, with only three of the\ncountably many paths shown. Each new edge has length $\\ell/2$.\nThe old endpoints remain at distance $\\ell$, and any two distinct new\nmiddle vertices also have distance $\\ell$. The d",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\draw[thick] (0,0)--node[above] {$\\ell$}(2,0);\n \\fill (0,0) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};\n \\fill (2,0) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};\n \\draw[-{Stealth},thick] (2.8,0)--(4,0);\n \\coordinate (u) at (5,0); \\coordinate (v) at (9,0);\n \\foreach \\h/\\lab in {1.4/1,0.45/2,-1.4/j}{\n   \\draw[thick] (u)--(7,\\h)--(v);\n   \\fill (7,\\h) circle (2pt) node[above] {$w_{\\lab}$};\n }\n \\node at (5.75,1.0) {$\\ell/2$};\n \\node at (8.25,1.0) {$\\ell/2$};\n \\node at (7,-0.5) {$\\vdots$};\n \\fill (u) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};\n \\fill (v) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};\n \\node at (1,-1.9) {one old edge};\n \\node at (7,-1.9) {countably many two-edge paths};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A replacement step diagram showing on the left a single horizontal edge connecting endpoint vertices $u$ and $v$ with label $\\ell$ above and label \"one old edge\" below. A horizontal rightward arrow connects this to the right diagram, which shows endpoints $u$ and $v$ connected by multiple two-edge paths via intermediate vertices $w_1$ (top), $w_2$ (middle), vertical ellipsis dots, and $w_j$ (bottom). The edges connecting $u$ to $w_1$ and $w_1$ to $v$ are each labeled $\\ell/2$. Below the right diagram is the label \"countably many two-edge paths\"."
    }

      "explanation": "The later result establishes a sharper lower bound on the distortion of diamond graph embeddings and broadens the target spaces beyond the single reflexive tree space.",

      "source_anchor": "met:joint-distortion",

      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both works operate on the finite height segment forest dual and the coordinate predual by decomposing vectors across finite ancestral subgraphs with contractive coordinate projections.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-dir-atomic-atoms",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-331.3",
      "source_quote": "The spaces $J$ and $X$ are reflexive, and finite coordinate arrays are dense in both. If a set $B\\subset T$ meets each segment in either a segment or the empty set, its coordinate projection $P_B$ is contractive on both spaces.",
      "target_anchor": "mol:prop:joint-budget",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-331.2",
      "target_quote": "For every finite ancestral $H$, every $x,y\\in Y$ with $x=P_Hx$, and every $R\\ge0$ with $\\|x+y\\|,\\|x-y\\|\\le R$",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The diamond graph distortion lower bound relies on the uniform displacement control established for both the forest dual and the infinite predual.",
      "relation": "uses",

      "source_pm_id": "PM-331.3",
      "source_quote": "into either $X$ or $B_\\infty$ satisfies",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main-tail",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-331.2",
      "target_quote": "Let $Y$ be either the finite-height forest dual $X=J^*$ or the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\\infty$.",
